ASML · ASML-NL · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: ASML

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 48,811 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

A881st of 49

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

ASML's external change picture is unusually strong and unusually concentrated in time. The demand change is real, evidenced by customers' committed capital and non-refundable down payments, and it carries more than two years of visibility because ASML's own supply caps the pace. The adverse forces are three: export-control policy (bounded at about a fifth of revenue, with servicing as the hidden exposure), supply capacity (a ceiling on units that also lengthens the pricing window) and the financing of AI capex together with the discount rate (which acts first on the multiple). What changed in the last six months against the prior twelve to eighteen: guidance up by about 25 percentage points of growth, the demand horizon extended from 2027 to 2028 and beyond, High-NA moved from doubt to production, and the MATCH Act moved closer to law. The modelling steer is to treat 2026 to 2027 as contracted, 2028 as probable but conditional on supply and financing, and 2029 as genuinely uncertain.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0246C-0544C-0740C-0948C-3582C-3938
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C+4613th of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is unchanged where the profit is (EUV, installed base, leading-edge immersion) and rising slowly at the periphery (mature-node DUV inside China, advanced-packaging lithography); the direction is a mild tightening that touches perhaps a tenth of revenue and none of the growth leg.

  • EUV. ASML remains the sole commercial supplier; nothing in the last six months changes that within three years. Chinese EUV claims (Langdao Silex's light source of more than 15 watts against the 500 to 600 watts commercial systems need) are judged political signalling by independent experts.
  • China DUV. Shanghai Aishengna (integrating Yuliangsheng and SMEE technology) began mass production of immersion DUV in July 2026, planning about five units in 2026 and 20 in 2027, at estimated yields of 10 to 20% and 10 to 30 wafers an hour against ASML's roughly 200. Against more than 300 ASML DUV systems a year, the near-term effect is China pricing and share at the margin, not global displacement. A former ASML senior manager puts production-worthy domestic immersion tools at 2029 to 2030.
  • Japanese competitors. Nikon plans 29 lithography units in its fiscal 2027 (27 in fiscal 2026) and is developing a mask-compatible immersion second source (prototype 2028, ramp fiscal 2031); its new DSP-110 digital lithography system (April 2027) targets large advanced-packaging substrates. Canon is gaining in KrF, i-line and back-end packaging, and nanoimprint is entering production-like evaluations. Yen weakness (about 31.5% over five years) lets both price mature tools aggressively.
  • Adjacent process competitors. Lam (dry resist, 10% dose reduction) and Tokyo Electron (gas-cluster etch reducing multi-patterning steps) attack lithography steps indirectly, and Lam grows advanced-packaging revenue about 70%. This is wallet-share competition, not head-to-head.
  • Peer read. ASM International, BESI and AIXTRON all face bundling or Chinese substitution in their own niches; ASML's exposure is the narrowest in the cohort because EUV is export-controlled away from China.

For the model: no change to EUV share or pricing assumptions; allow for slower DUV growth and some China DUV price pressure from 2027 (consensus DUV growth of +25% and +21% already sits below the +31% and +29% immersion capacity growth).

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C4417th of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: inputs are a volume ceiling rather than a cost problem; the net effect is a headwind to unit upside in 2027 to 2028 and to the pace of gross-margin expansion (capacity-ramp costs), and a tailwind to pricing duration and visibility. On balance slightly negative for the next eighteen months.

  • Availability. The binding constraints have shifted. At Q3 the chief executive said Carl Zeiss is "not currently a bottleneck"; Deutsche Bank names ASML's own module-build capacity (Wilton, Taiwan) and the wider specialist supply chain (precision stages, frames, gas systems, Eindhoven-area machine shops) as the dependencies for raising 2028 output. Experts still cite optics and installation as the tightest points. 2027 output is fixed by long-lead parts; any increase is a 2028 event.
  • Concentration. Sole sources remain: Zeiss optics (24.9% owned; interest-free loans; maximum exposure about 3.9 billion EUR), TRUMPF lasers, Gudeng pods; 272 of 5,100 suppliers carry 89% of product spend. The annual report states ASML would "cease to conduct business" if Zeiss supply stopped.
  • Raw materials and geopolitics. Helium (Ras Laffan strikes in March 2026; about one week of working inventory), Chinese rare-earth controls (suspension to 10 November 2026) and Chinese sanctions on a German supplier (July 2026) show that political conflict reaches ASML through suppliers.
  • Cost. Supplier inflation is managed through procurement pressure and pricing (system pricing reported up 3 to 6% while ASML pushes cost-down); frame contracts with fixed schedules raise supplier visibility. But management guided Q4 2026 GM about 50 basis points below Q3 despite higher revenue because capacity-ramp and supply-chain investment costs absorb part of the volume and pricing benefit, and said part of the value-based pricing gain will fund capacity.

For the model: cap Low-NA units at about 85 in 2027; treat 2028 units above about 110 as an upside scenario tied to supplier and module-build milestones; deduct a capacity-ramp cost (tens of basis points of GM) from the pricing benefit in 2027 to 2028.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C-4021st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the macro force acting now is the discount rate combined with the financing of the AI build-out; it bites hardest at the sector valuation level (semicap multiples), much less at the industry level, and least on ASML's near-term fundamentals, which are prepaid. The fundamental transmission, if it comes, runs through 2028 to 2029 orders.

  • The regime. The Fitness for Fighting digest of 23 October 2026 reads risk-off confirmed and broadening: US 10-year near 5.10% with a December hike above 90% priced, Brent at 111 US dollars, French sovereign stress spreading to Italy, technology net selling the heaviest since January 2025, and Momentum and Semiconductors tilted Underweight with Fragility at Double Underweight.
  • AI financing. The AI theme "cracked at its financing, not its demand": CoreWeave bonds below 78 cents, neocloud and hyperscaler spreads wider, Oracle and SoftBank sold as levered AI plays. Jefferies says hyperscaler FCF troughs near zero in 2026 to 2027 and the next leg of a 5.4 trillion US dollar build must be financed externally; about a third of hyperscaler capex depends on external financing (Deutsche Bank). No house found slowing compute demand.
  • Level at which it bites. Sector: European semicap names broke technically in October; ASML's technical momentum fell from strongest to a break in a week; its multiple sits at the 2nd percentile of five years. Industry: technology rotates as halves (analogue up, equipment down per Jefferies). Company fundamentals: 2027 deliveries are funded by non-refundable down payments, so a financing scare first changes the multiple and only later, if it persists into 2027, the 2028 to 2029 order book.
  • Other macro channels. Memory prices (semiconductor sales +144% year on year in August, price-driven; growth expected to peak about October 2026 on base effects); currency (euro billing protects reported earnings, but a stronger euro, forecast at 1.15 to 1.23 against the dollar, raises tool costs for dollar-funded customers); higher input inflation.

For the model: no direct cut to 2026 to 2027; a cost-of-capital and AI-financing scenario should flex 2028 to 2029 units and the exit multiple rather than near-term revenue.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–48

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other external dynamics matter: the physical readiness of customers' fabs (cleanrooms, power, labour), the financing structures that sit between customers and ASML, and the people constraint in the Netherlands. Together they are timing risks rather than demand risks, and two of them also support ASML's high-margin upgrade business.

  • Customer fab readiness. Cleanroom construction (about a year), power availability and skilled construction labour gate installations. The chief executive says ASML checks requests against fab plans and that clean-room constraints move individual shipments by "days or weeks"; the CFO warns the scale of 2027 to 2028 customer expansion needs unusually disciplined construction. The same constraint is why customers buy productivity upgrades now (a reinforcing loop for IBM margin while it lasts).
  • Financing between customer and supplier. Customers fund down payments partly through bank factoring (6.2 billion EUR of receivables derecognised in FY2025, an outlier against Applied Materials' 324 million US dollars); new customers such as Terafab appear in contract liabilities. If credit conditions tighten, the cost of these structures rises for customers; it is not an ASML balance-sheet risk but it is a demand-timing risk.
  • People and housing. A former ASML researcher argues the plan to add roughly 20,000 workplaces could slip toward 2030 on engineering scarcity and Dutch housing; an engineering director reports constraints in ASML's application-support and installation capacity being addressed by hiring. ASML responded with a conditional 20,000 EUR share award to eligible employees (about 900 million EUR in aggregate) vesting 1 January 2030.
  • Geopolitical bargaining. European executives argue EU states may use ASML's systems as bargaining chips with the US; Europe's own fab build lags (ASML executive Frank Heemskerk, September 2026).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A821st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: adjacent entrants are a real but China-only threat to mature DUV; standard entrants are impossible; orthogonal entrants are long-dated options with low probability of mattering within four years.

  • Adjacent. Shanghai Aishengna, SMEE and Huawei-linked programmes (immersion DUV mass production from July 2026; five and 20 units planned for 2026 and 2027). Threat: China DUV pricing and share, and, through the policy loop, a higher chance of export bans. Moderate for China, negligible globally.
  • Standard. None. Annual R&D of about 4.7 billion EUR (14.4% of sales), the Zeiss optical lock and decades of system integration make vanilla entry impossible.
  • Orthogonal or innovative. xLight free-electron laser (150 million US dollar CHIPS award; prototype at Albany by 2028; less than 50% chance of US-fab deployment by 2030; it would retrofit into ASML systems, threatening TRUMPF's role more than ASML's); Substrate X-ray lithography (100 million US dollars raised; a decade-long maturity gap); Canon nanoimprint (defects; aimed at memory and packaging); Langdao Silex EUV source (more than 15 watts, far below commercial needs). Low threat within the horizon.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D3834th of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers behave as captive partners and are a support by contract and a risk by construction; their behaviour over the last six months has improved (Zeiss "not currently a bottleneck", frame contracts with fixed schedules), but their scalability is now the binding constraint on 2028 upside, and single sources remain catastrophic in the tail.

  • Support. "Two companies, one business" with Zeiss SMT (24.9% owned; 10-year capex loans with a 3-year grace period and interest capped at 1% or interest-free; advances to secure optical columns); 60-day payment terms; ASML presses suppliers for cost-downs (suppliers report margin squeeze); about 80% of the supply base near European hubs; secondary suppliers being added for non-critical parts.
  • Risk. Sole sources (Zeiss optics, TRUMPF lasers, Gudeng pods); zero fungibility between Low-NA and High-NA optics; specialist machine shops and gas-system suppliers needing investment; geopolitical exposure (Chinese sanctions on a German supplier; rare earths; helium); supplier financial health not disclosed.
  • Economics. Suppliers capture far lower margins (optics 15 to 20% EBIT estimated; mechatronics 13 to 16.5%) than ASML (about 45% EBIT now), which confirms where power sits; but supplier inflation is only partly recoverable and capacity-ramp costs are absorbing part of the pricing benefit.

For the model: suppliers do not threaten the base case; they cap the upside case for 2028 units and add a cost line to the ramp.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0188C-0366C-0430C-0890C-3285C-3728C-4382
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

A881st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand has changed sharply upward over the last six months, as a cyclical surge with more than two years of visibility layered on a structural rise in lithography intensity; visibility is high for 2027, medium-high for 2028 and qualitative only for 2029 to 2030.

  • Magnitude of the change. FY2026 revenue guidance moved from 34 to 39 billion EUR (January) to 36 to 40 (April) to 43 to 45 (July) to 44 to 45 (October): the out-turn is about +36% against an initial midpoint of about +11%. Q3 2026 revenue rose 55.2% year on year. At Q3 the chief executive said customers expect semiconductor supply to remain below demand "through at least 2028" and that discussions have moved to 2029 and 2030 orders.
  • Evidence at the customer, not only the company. TSMC raised 2026 capital expenditure to 60 to 64 billion US dollars (from 52 to 56); Morgan Stanley expects 75 to 80 billion in 2027. SK hynix, Samsung and Micron are building (M15X, P5, Yongin Y2, ID1) and memory makers sign long-term agreements with deposits. WFE is forecast at about 145 to 152 billion US dollars in 2026, 193 billion in 2027 and 220 billion in 2028.
  • Structural component. DRAM EUV layers rise from three exposures at 1b to five or six at 1c and eight at 1d (DRAM EUV exposures forecast up 4.5 times in five years); logic layer counts rise from about 20 (N3) to about 22 (N2), so logic intensity is close to flat at N2 and rises again at A14 and A10 (25 to 30 Low-NA-equivalent layers by 2030). The structural driver is therefore memory-led in the near term.
  • Cyclical component. The 2026 surge is memory-led (memory system sales about +75% in 2026, logic about +10%) and partly a stock of deferred demand, plus a China re-acceleration in Q3 (23% of system sales after 14% in Q2). Memory pricing is the classic cyclical variable; Jefferies models the semiconductor cycle peaking around Q4 2026 on price base effects.
  • Visibility. 2027: high. Low-NA capacity of about 85 systems is fixed by long-lead parts and "close to fully covered" by orders; non-current CL rose to 9.3 billion EUR at Q3 from 3.4 billion; Bernstein infers a backlog of about 56 billion EUR against the last disclosed 38.8 billion. 2028: medium-high. Orders "increasing", but no unit plan, no disclosed bookings. 2029 to 2030: interest, not orders; ASML has not accepted all requests and China is excluded from those EUV discussions.
  • Fragility. The demand stack is increasingly memory- and AI-capex-dependent, and AI capex is now being financed externally (hyperscaler FCF troughs near zero in 2026 to 2027 per Jefferies; neocloud credit stress). Morgan Stanley sees hyperscaler and neocloud capex growth decelerating to 14% in 2028. This does not threaten 2027 deliveries, which are prepaid, but it is the variable that would decide whether 2028 to 2029 orders firm.

For the model: treat 2026 to 2027 demand as contracted (units capped by supply), 2028 as probable but supply- and financing-conditional, and 2029 as a scenario variable with a wide cone.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

A661st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external disruption forces against ASML's core are being disproved over the three-year horizon, and the main "disruption" debate of the last eighteen months (that High-NA would be a commercial failure) is being disproved by customer milestones; the residual disruption risks act on the High-NA leg and on long-term lithography intensity, not on the 2027 to 2028 model, and one is internal (software upgrades substituting for new tools).

  • High-NA moving from doubt to production. Intel has processed more than one million wafers on High-NA with yields matching Low-NA on dual-qualified layers; Samsung (DRAM) and SK hynix plan high-volume High-NA in 2028; TSMC plans about 2030 (A10). Fleet availability approaches 90%. The "Airbus A380" framing from earlier boutique research is losing force. The 12-inch photomask initiative (ASML, TSMC, now Samsung; pilot 2031, production 2033, more than 40% productivity gain for dual-mask exposure) attacks the stitching penalty that limited High-NA in large logic dies.
  • Substitutes disproved for leading-edge volume. Nanoimprint (defects), the xLight free-electron laser (less than 50% chance of US-fab deployment by 2030 per Jefferies, and it would sit inside ASML scanners), Substrate's X-ray approach (a decade-long subsystem gap), Chinese EUV (far from integrated systems).
  • Partial bypasses that remain live. (i) GAA logic shifting critical-dimension control toward deposition and etch (N2 roughly flat on EUV layers versus N3); (ii) advanced packaging and hybrid bonding shifting capital toward deposition, etch and plating; (iii) 3D DRAM eventually limiting EUV layers (a 2030-plus question; Bernstein notes delays to 2032 would extend lateral lithography intensity); (iv) Low-NA multi-patterning delaying High-NA at TSMC.
  • Internal substitution. Software and firmware upgrades deliver 5 to 7% throughput more cheaply than new hardware. This cannibalises a slice of new-tool demand in the short run while carrying far higher margin; it is a mix and timing effect rather than a demand loss.

Direction for the case: a tailwind on the core over three years; a modest headwind on the timing of High-NA revenue (consensus carries only about 1.4 billion EUR a year of High-NA revenue in FY2026 and FY2027; Jefferies models eight systems in 2028).

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3046th of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political and regulatory change is the single most adverse external force, it has intensified in the last six months, and it bears on about a fifth of revenue (China) and on the high-margin servicing of the Chinese installed base; it does not touch the EUV growth leg. The next six months carry two dated decision points.

  • What already binds. Dutch licences cover EUV and the NXT:1970i and 1980i immersion tools (integrated into the EU control list in November 2025); upgrades that lift overlay or throughput by more than 1% on Chinese tools are prohibited; US rules restrict US-person support for advanced Chinese fabs. China fell from 40.5% of system revenue in 2024 to 32.2% in 2025 (29.1% of total sales) and is guided at about 20% of 2026 revenue.
  • What is new. The bipartisan MATCH Act (introduced April 2026) would ban immersion shipments, servicing and technical support to designated Chinese facilities (covered affiliates of SMIC, Hua Hong, Huawei, CXMT, YMTC) and gives allies 150 days to follow. The sector digest now places it "in the year-end defence bill", which raises its passage probability relative to the earlier "standalone passage unlikely" reading by Deutsche Bank and Hana. Separately, the US suspension of the 50% Affiliates Rule and China's suspension of rare-earth export controls both expire on 10 November 2026 unless extended.
  • Size. Jefferies estimates about 15% of revenue at risk and models a MATCH-case FY2027 of about 54 billion EUR revenue and about 44 EUR EPS against its central 61 billion and 64 EUR. The installed-base exposure is the under-appreciated part: more than 1,400 installed DUV tools in China generate service and upgrade revenue, and servicing bans or remote software restrictions would hit IBM, the highest-margin line.
  • Offsets. Sovereign subsidies (US CHIPS equity stakes, EU Chips Act 2.0 targeting 120 billion EUR, Korea's 880 billion US dollar decade plan) are redirecting fab construction to Taiwan, Korea, the US and Japan; Jefferies argues halted Chinese capacity would largely migrate elsewhere over time. ASML's China share (16% of H1 2026 revenue) is already the lowest among WFE peers (Applied 27%, Lam 30%, Tokyo Electron 29%), so the relative exposure is smaller than its peers'.
  • Second-order loop. A credible domestic Chinese immersion tool lowers Washington's political cost of a ban, and a ban accelerates Chinese qualification of domestic tools: a reinforcing loop against ASML's China DUV franchise over three to five years.

For the model: carry a base case with China at about 20% of 2026 revenue and roughly 4 to 5 billion EUR of annual China system revenue from 2027 (Huatai), plus an explicit MATCH scenario that removes China immersion systems and a share of China IBM from 2027.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

A901st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the AI-driven WFE up-cycle, and within it rising lithography intensity in DRAM and leading-edge logic, is accelerating ASML's demand directly and more than any other European equipment name's, because ASML sits at the point of the chain where AI capex converts into tool orders with the fewest intermediaries.

  • The driver in numbers. WFE about 145 to 152 billion US dollars in 2026 (+16% to +23.5%), 193 billion in 2027, 220 billion in 2028 (Deutsche Bank; Morgan Stanley's global team is higher at 191 billion in 2027). Lithography intensity (lithography as a share of WFE) is modelled by Redburn at about 24.9% of a 272 billion US dollar WFE market by 2030.
  • Transmission into ASML. Memory system sales +75% in 2026 (HBM and 1c DRAM, new EUV adopters such as Nanya); EUV revenue growth above 45% in 2026; non-EUV about +20%; IBM about +50%. TSMC's N2 capacity compound growth of about 70% a year (2026 to 2028) and the 1.4nm Fab 25 (April 2027) pull logic tools forward. TSMC reported HPC revenue at 68% of sales and saw no slowdown in AI demand (October 2026).
  • Why ASML gains more than peers. EUV share is 100% where the incremental intensity lands; China is excluded from EUV, so the growth mix is free of the restricted geography; DUV immersion is pulled along at roughly a one-to-one capital ratio with EUV in advanced fabs.
  • Limits. Advanced packaging growth accrues mainly to deposition, etch and bonding peers (Lam, BESI, ASM); logic EUV layer counts are roughly flat N3 to N2; and the driver is now visibly dependent on financing.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A851st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand exceeds supply and customers are paying in advance to secure 2027 to 2028 capacity, which supports any growth thesis strongly; the support is fragile in concentration (a handful of customers and two countries) and in its dependence on memory pricing and AI financing.

  • Support. 2027 Low-NA capacity close to fully ordered; substantial 2028 orders; 2029 to 2030 requests partly accepted; non-current CL up about 5 to 6 billion EUR in Q3; memory customers sign long-term agreements with deposits; customers buy software upgrades to add capacity while waiting for tools.
  • Concentration. Four customers were 61.2% of FY2025 revenue (20.0 billion EUR); Q3 2026 system sales were 32% Taiwan, 31% Korea, 23% China, 13% US. A single customer's capex freeze (TSMC was about 31% of a peer's revenue; TSMC's share of ASML is similarly large) or a regional shock would hit at once.
  • Composition shift. Memory is driving growth (memory system sales +75% in 2026 against logic +10%), which raises exposure to DRAM pricing; Jefferies flags memory capex pullback risk from late 2027.
  • Behavioural risk. Long lead times encourage precautionary booking; ASML's controls (down payments with exit costs, demand checked against fab plans, selective acceptance of 2029 to 2030 requests) reduce but do not remove it.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F2845th of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and politicians are the least supportive actors in ASML's chain and can move revenue by decree; on balance they hurt (China shipments and servicing) while partly helping through subsidies that build fabs elsewhere. The position is fragile because the decisive variables (the MATCH Act, Dutch licences, US-China truce terms) are outside management's control and have dated triggers in November and December 2026.

  • Hurting. Dutch licensing for EUV and advanced immersion; EU-wide control list; a 1% cap on throughput or overlay upgrades for installed Chinese tools; US person-support restrictions; the MATCH Act's proposed ban on immersion shipments and servicing; remote software lockout as a theoretical lever (experts note tools require active software licensing).
  • Helping. US CHIPS subsidies and equity stakes (Intel 10% for 5.7 billion US dollars), EU Chips Act 2.0, Korea's 880 billion US dollar decade plan, China's own state funding for mature DUV that local makers cannot yet supply.
  • Two-sided. Technology sovereignty creates redundant regional fabs (more tools) while fragmenting standards and duplicating capacity (a later digestion risk).
  • Fragility markers. 10 November 2026 (expiry of the US Affiliates Rule suspension and China's rare-earth suspension); year-end US defence bill (MATCH Act vehicle); Dutch licence approval rates visible through ASML's China share each quarter.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

B+826th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is large enough to support mid-teens to low-twenties percent annual revenue growth from 2025 to 2030, well above the framework's 12 to 20% band, with the company's own 2030 framework (44 to 60 billion EUR) now an under-statement of what the market allows.

  • WFE. About 145 to 152 billion US dollars in 2026, 193 billion in 2027, 220 billion in 2028 (forecasts); Redburn models about 272 billion by 2030 with lithography at about 24.9% (about 68 billion US dollars).
  • Segments. Lithography spending growth: DRAM 15 to 25% a year and advanced logic 10 to 20% a year over 2025 to 2030 (management's frame); IBM targeted at 11 to 13 billion EUR by 2030 at the 2024 Investor Day, already about 12 billion EUR in 2026, with Bernstein at about 23 billion EUR by 2030; packaging lithography 100 to 150 billion yen by 2028 to 2030; metrology growing (YieldStar demand more than doubling).
  • Implied growth. Bernstein models a 25% revenue compound rate 2025 to 2030; consensus implies about 26% a year from FY2025 to FY2029 (32.7 to 83.6 billion EUR), although FY2029 rests on eight brokers.
  • Constraints on using the TAM. Supply (30% a year capacity growth), China access (about a fifth of revenue), and the cycle.

For the model: the TAM does not bind before 2030; supply and the cycle do.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0682C-1084
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

A821st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the company is in the acceleration phase of an up-cycle that turned in late 2025; revenue growth is still accelerating, but EPS growth peaks in 2026 and decelerates thereafter on consensus, so the second derivative, which drives semicap multiples, has already turned down.

  • The turn. Year-on-year quarterly revenue growth: +0.7% (Q3 2025), +4.9% (Q4 2025), +13.2% (Q1 2026), +21.3% (Q2 2026), +55.2% (Q3 2026). Trailing-twelve-month revenue to Q3 2026 is about 39.5 billion EUR, +22.5% on the prior year, now above the company's 2019 to 2025 normal of about 18% a year.
  • Where it sits. Revenue growth on consensus is +36% (FY2026), +29% (FY2027), +28% (FY2028), +13.5% (FY2029); EPS growth +72%, +35%, +33%, +20%. The supply cap smooths the shape: capacity rises about 30% a year, so revenue cannot spike and therefore cannot collapse as sharply as in an unconstrained cycle, provided orders hold.
  • Company base rate. Growth years have come in pairs and triples followed by digestion (FY2021 +33% then +14%; FY2023 +30% then +2.6%). The present cycle, if consensus is right, would be the longest run of above-20% growth in the company's history (2026 to 2028). That is plausible because it is capacity-paced and prepaid, but it is outside the base rate.
  • Is it turning? Not on orders: orders now reach 2028 with interest for 2029 to 2030, and consensus FY2027 EPS was revised up 7% in the month after Q3. It is turning in two softer senses: the growth rate of EPS peaks this year, and the composition of growth is shifting from upgrade- and margin-led (2026) to unit- and price-led (2027 to 2028).

For the model: 2026 is the peak EPS-growth year; 2027 growth is price, mix and IBM-led on fixed units; 2028 adds units; 2029 is the first year with a genuine probability of digestion.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

A841st of 49

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is the strongest fundamental tailwind in the European equipment universe sitting inside the weakest market regime for its sector. At value-chain level the tailwinds dominate decisively; at industry level they dominate; at sector (stock-market) level the headwinds currently dominate the multiple. For an 18-month to four-year operating model the tailwinds govern; for the share price over the next six months the headwinds have the upper hand.

Thematic drivers taken in turn:

  • AI infrastructure capex (tailwind, strong; financing a headwind to the multiple). Demand readings intact; financing and credit are the crack.
  • Memory up-cycle and HBM (tailwind, strong; cyclical peak risk 2027 to 2028). The fastest-growing part of ASML's demand and its most cyclical.
  • Node transitions and lithography intensity (tailwind, moderate to strong). DRAM layers rising fast, logic flat at N2 then rising, High-NA becoming production technology.
  • Sovereign technology funding (tailwind, moderate). A floor under fab construction outside China.
  • China localisation and export controls (headwind, moderate and intensifying). About a fifth of revenue and a slice of IBM exposed; dated triggers in November and December 2026.
  • Advanced packaging and alternative scaling (mild headwind). Shifts wallet share to deposition, etch and bonding.
  • Discount rate and energy-led inflation (headwind to valuation, strong at present).
  • Supply chain (mixed). Caps volume upside, extends pricing power and visibility.

All together. Value chain: a monopoly at the gate of a customer-funded build-out, with orders reaching 2028 and interest to 2030; headwinds confined to policy and supply. Industry (semiconductor production equipment): a WFE up-cycle forecast to run through 2028 with record backlogs across peers. Sector (European semiconductors in the market): Underweight in a risk-off regime, de-rating before the feared 2028 peak. The one connection the Consumer should carry: the headwinds act mainly on the multiple and on 2028 to 2029 timing, while the tailwinds act on the 2026 to 2028 earnings line. The model's cone should therefore be narrow for 2026 to 2027 and widen sharply from 2028.

Internal change forces/risks

A802nd of 49

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal forces amplify the external surge and convert it into higher-quality earnings: more units where supply allows, higher prices from 2027, more software-rich installed-base revenue now, and a falling share count. Leadership is stable and credible; strategy has shifted from planning growth to removing the supply constraint; guidance has been raised repeatedly with the mid-term framework visibly stale ahead of the June 2027 CMD. The negatives are a deliberate lack of quantification (2028 units, pricing size, 2027 margin, bookings) and early High-NA and capacity-ramp costs that make the margin path less linear than the Q3 print suggests. For the model this means revenue can grow faster than units from 2027, gross margin expansion slows after 2026 before pricing lands fully in 2028, and capital returns are larger than the announced programme.

Risks

B559th of 49

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is benign on the company's own operations and serious on exogenous fronts. The growth engine is accelerating, management is strong, estimates are rising and the balance sheet is net cash. The material risks are a statutory China ban (plausible, about 15% of revenue, with servicing as the hidden part), a cycle or financing shock to 2028 to 2029 orders, and the low-probability catastrophes (Taiwan; Zeiss). Perception risk is high in the current regime and has already cut the multiple to near its trough, so the remaining share-price downside is mostly earnings-led. For the model: carry three explicit scenarios (MATCH case removing China immersion and part of China IBM from 2027; a 2029 digestion with revenue flat to down 10%; a severe downturn with FY2028 EPS in the high 30s to mid 40s EUR) beside the base case.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C+6213th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: execution standards are improving and already high (record margins, High-NA availability near 90%, faster cycle times); cost discipline is real but its savings arrive later and smaller than first announced; governance is sound but disclosure has narrowed. Net, a moderate positive.

  • Execution. Q3 2026 operating margin 44.4% (32.8% a year earlier); 49 EUV systems recognised in nine months, on track for 65 Low-NA in 2026; High-NA fleet availability near 90% and maturity ahead of Low-NA at the same stage; AI diagnostics cut wafer-stage failure analysis from ten hours to eight minutes; module-based shipping.
  • Cost discipline. The January 2026 delayering targeted a net reduction of about 1,700 positions (about 3,000 management and coordination roles out, 1,300 to 1,400 engineering roles in), with ING estimating about 200 million EUR a year of savings and 0.5 to 1.0 points of EBIT margin. Union negotiations reduced redundancies and deferred them to May 2027. At group scale (operating expenses of about 6.4 billion EUR a year) the saving is small; its value is decision speed.
  • Operating expense trend. Q3 R&D about 1.2 billion EUR and SG&A about 0.4 billion EUR, broadly in line with guidance; operating expenses grew far slower than revenue (about 61% drop-through on FY2026 incremental revenue on my arithmetic).
  • Governance and disclosure. Clean first-year PwC audit; fully independent Supervisory Board. But quarterly bookings are no longer disclosed (annual backlog only), and at Q3 ASML gave no new unit plan, pricing percentage or 2027 margin, which reduces the frequency and precision with which the case can be tracked.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

A504th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negative internal developments are second-order individually but cluster around one theme: the 2027 to 2028 earnings path depends on things management has chosen not to quantify. Five are present and two are plausible.

Present:

  • Under-quantification. No 2028 unit target, no pricing percentage, no normalised upgrade run-rate, no 2027 GM; quarterly bookings discontinued. Investors are asked to value a more aggressive ambition before it can be verified.
  • Margin offsets. Q4 2026 GM guided at about 57%, below Q3's 57.5% on higher revenue, because of capacity-ramp costs, supply-chain investment, High-NA mix and product mix; management named prolonged reliance on the NXE:3800F as a further offset.
  • High-NA economics. Morgan Stanley estimates early High-NA GM at about 20% against a group 57.5%; four to five recognitions in 2026 at roughly 350 to 400 million EUR each would dilute group GM by perhaps 70 to 100 basis points on my arithmetic.
  • Earnings quality noise. The Q3 Mistral revaluation (509.7 million EUR) flatters reported EPS; R&D in the statutory (IFRS) accounts rose to 2.5 billion EUR in H1 2026 from 1.8 billion, and IFRS development amortisation depressed the IFRS GM to 50.9% in H1 2026.
  • Organisational friction. The delayering was scaled back and deferred during a 30%-plus ramp; employee engagement (78%) is 2.3 points below the top-quartile benchmark; installation and application-support capacity is a reported constraint.

Plausible:

  • Capacity built at the top of the cycle. Gross inventories were 12.2 billion EUR at end-2025; the filings warn of write-downs if demand does not match the build.
  • Buybacks at elevated prices funded by customer money. If orders were deferred, cash would reverse before the profit and loss account did, and buyback intensity would have to fall.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

A784th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the culture is engineering-led, direct and broadly congruent with the strategy (technology leadership, customer co-development, value pricing), with low attrition and high pride; its incongruence was bureaucratic complexity from rapid growth, which the 2026 delayering addresses at the cost of short-term friction.

  • Positives: attrition 3.9% in 2025 (4.2% in 2024; internal limit 7%); number-one employer brand in the Netherlands (2024); "low-context", direct communication; engagement 78%, within about two points of the top-quartile benchmark.
  • Strain: headcount rose from about 30,800 (2021) to above 44,500 (2026), diluting cohesion; employees reported that several managers could steer a single task; the reorganisation to product-centric module teams is the response.
  • Alignment mechanisms: variable pay about 80% of CEO target pay; long-term incentives weighted 35% relative TSR and 35% ROAIC; shareholding guidelines met; societal pay-ratio benchmarking with the Works Council.
  • Comparison with the framework's exemplars (Lifco, Avanza, Ryanair): good but not yet proven as a total system through a downturn under this leadership.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D4223rd of 49

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: five sudden fundamental risks stand out; one is plausible within the model horizon (a statutory China ban), two are low probability but very large (a Taiwan Strait event; a sole-source supply outage), and two are medium-term cyclical shocks with a sudden trigger (an AI-capex financing freeze; a memory price break). Several are correlated, which makes their joint downside larger than the sum of their separate probabilities suggests.

  1. MATCH Act or equivalent statutory ban on immersion shipments and servicing to China. Plausibility: meaningful within 12 to 18 months (bipartisan; now reportedly in the year-end defence bill; Deutsche Bank and Hana judge standalone passage unlikely). Size: about 15% of revenue at risk (Jefferies), concentrated in high-margin DUV and IBM; Jefferies' MATCH case puts FY2027 at about 54 billion EUR revenue and about 44 EUR EPS against 61 billion and 64 EUR. Correlated with Chinese domestic tool progress and with rare-earth retaliation (truce terms expire 10 November 2026).
  2. Taiwan Strait conflict or blockade. Low probability, catastrophic size: Taiwan was 25.5% of 2025 sales and 32% of Q3 2026 system sales, and a conflict would disrupt the whole semiconductor supply chain and ASML's customer facilities.
  3. Sole-source supply outage (Zeiss optics, TRUMPF lasers). Very low probability, catastrophic size: the annual report says ASML would cease to conduct business if Zeiss supply stopped. Related lesser shocks: helium (one week of working inventory), rare earths, Chinese sanctions on suppliers.
  4. AI-capex financing freeze. Low to medium probability within 18 months, large size: hyperscaler FCF near zero in 2026 to 2027 and external financing needs of trillions; Redburn's scenario of a 2028 "return reckoning" with sudden capex freezes. Effect on ASML: push-outs of 2028 to 2029 orders and a de-rating; 2027 deliveries are prepaid.
  5. Memory price break. Medium probability from H2 2027, large size: memory is about half of 2026 system sales; a DRAM spot price fall below peer cash costs would trigger capex cuts (Morgan Stanley; Jefferies). The risks research judges the compound of a DRAM downturn and China localisation low near term and rising beyond 24 months. - Also noted: a customer-specific capex freeze (TSMC cut of more than 10% is the threshold the earnings review uses); High-NA qualification failure (slow, not sudden); a tax-regime change (Pillar Two or the innovation box; sudden but modest).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B6011th of 49

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers are concentrated, and concentrated in a way that is favourable for the 18-month window and unfavourable beyond it. Three variables explain about two-thirds of the revenue difference between FY2025 and FY2028 and almost all of the margin difference: Low-NA unit output, Low-NA price and mix, and the upgrade component of IBM. A fourth, China DUV under policy, is the swing. Through FY2027 two of the three are contracted (units fixed and ordered; prices signed), so concentration makes the forecast more predictable than a diffuse one would be; from FY2028 the same three variables are uncontracted (units above 110, the size of the price flow-through, the speed of upgrade normalisation), so the same concentration makes the cone wide. The forecasts are, in effect, a bet on three numbers that the company will disclose in January and June 2027.

The arithmetic of the difference (my decomposition of the base case, FY2025 to FY2028). Revenue rises 37.0 billion EUR (32.7 to 69.7). Low-NA units at the FY2025 blended price (44 to 110 units at about 218 million EUR): about +14.4 billion EUR, 39% of the difference. Low-NA price and mix (218 to 274 million EUR on 110 units): about +6.2 billion EUR, 17%. High-NA (about 0.9 to 3.1 billion EUR): +2.3 billion EUR, 6%. Non-EUV systems including metrology (12.9 to 21.4 billion EUR): +8.5 billion EUR, 23%, of which immersion volume is most and China is a negative. IBM (8.2 to 15.0 billion EUR): +6.8 billion EUR, 18%, of which upgrades about +3.8 billion EUR and service about +3.0 billion EUR. So the top three (Low-NA units, Low-NA price and mix, IBM upgrades) are about 66%; add immersion volume and the four explain about 85%. For the margin: EBIT margin rises 12.6 points (34.6% to 47.2%), of which gross margin contributes 6.7 points (volume absorption and EUV mix about +3, upgrades net of their 2028 normalisation about +1, pricing net of ramp costs about +1.2, High-NA about -1, DUV and other about +2.5 on my reading of the second set's bridge) and operating-expense leverage 5.9 points. The operating-expense leverage is the one genuinely diffuse contributor: it is the arithmetic of revenue outrunning a fixed base of about 6.2 to 7.8 billion EUR, and it depends on nothing but the revenue line.

Against the last four to six years. FY2019 to FY2025 revenue growth (about 18.5% a year) was itself concentrated: EUV went from 2.8 to 11.6 billion EUR, immersion from 4.7 to 10.3 (with China's 2023 to 2024 surge), service and field options from 2.8 to 8.2. The difference now is not that the drivers have multiplied but that the same three have been compressed into a shorter period and have acquired a supply cap that fixes their near-term values. The 2019 to 2025 cone was wide because the drivers were demand-paced (customer push-outs in 2024, supply disruption in 2022); the 2026 to 2027 cone is narrow because they are supply-paced and prepaid.

What the concentration implies for predictability, year by year.

  • FY2027: units fixed (about 84 to 85), price signed (size unknown but direction certain), upgrades held near the 2026 run-rate by cleanroom scarcity. Residual uncertainty is in IBM (about ±1.5 billion EUR), China DUV (about ±2 billion EUR) and the GM translation (about ±1 point). Concentration helps.
  • FY2028: units 95 to 120 (the whole difference between a 20% miss and a 10% beat on EBIT), full pricing against an undisclosed percentage, upgrades falling at an unknown rate, and a possible statutory China ban. The same three drivers, now all open. Concentration hurts: the T-D2 report's reading of broker dispersion (FY2028 EPS from Morgan Stanley's 68.47 through Bernstein's 84 to Redburn's about 91; visible range about 53 to 90) is the market's own measure of how much depends on three numbers.
  • FY2029 and beyond: the drivers remain the same three plus the cycle; the T-D2 report's precedents (2001 and 2009: 20 points of gross-margin compression within three quarters of the utilisation peak; 2012 and 2019: forecasts resting on units and price "regularly missed") apply to the back half of the model, not to the 18-month window.

Interplay that compounds the concentration. The three drivers are not independent in a downturn: when cleanrooms open (loop 1 of the second set) upgrades fall and tool demand is served; when AI financing tightens, 2028 to 2029 units slip and down payments slow (loop 2); when a memory price break arrives, upgrades and DUV volume fall together. The second set's downside case (FY2028 EPS about 59 EUR, -22%) is the product of these correlations, which is why the FY2028 cone is skewed down even though each driver taken alone is roughly symmetrical. The upside is capped by supply on all three at once.

Peer contrast. Applied Materials' drivers are diffuse across seven equipment categories and a 23%-of-revenue service business, which is why its guidance swings less (T-I1); BESI's single driver (the hybrid-bonding insertion date) produced a 54% drawdown when it slipped eighteen months while earnings held; ASM International's content driver (gate-all-around layers) brought its 2030 target forward three years. ASML sits between Applied and BESI: three drivers, not one and not seven, with the near-term values of two of them fixed by its own supply chain.

For the memo (topic #9). The case is simple to state and to track: three numbers decide it. Simplicity is a virtue for the next 18 months and a fragility for months 18 to 36, and the memo should say both.

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5425th of 49

Judgement: there has been no change at the top in the last six months; the leadership changes of the past eighteen months were completions of a succession (a CTO added, the CFO and COO extended, the board refreshed), and they signal continuity of an execution-focused team through the 2026 to 2028 ramp. C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B+708th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has not been replaced but its priorities have intensified materially since mid-2026, from planning growth to converting demand into output, with the installed base promoted to a "capacity product" and pricing reset to value-based terms. The change is well articulated in direction and well resourced financially (by customers' cash), but under-quantified: no 2028 unit plan, no pricing percentage, no 2027 margin target.

  • Capacity. At Q2 2026 the plan was about 30% Low-NA EUV and immersion capacity growth in 2027 with another 30% under investigation for 2028 (about 65, 85 and 110 Low-NA systems; about 130, 170 and 220 immersion systems). At Q3 the chief executive said 30% in both years "will not meet customer demand" and that ASML is investigating more than 30% for 2028. Output is now one of his two stated daily priorities.
  • Multiple levers. Output growth is framed as factory floor space, shorter cycle times, supplier capacity, higher-throughput tools (NXE:3800F at 260 wafers an hour versus 230 for the 3800E; next-generation immersion at 330) and upgrades that add wafers inside existing fabs. This means revenue can grow faster than the number of scanners shipped.
  • Installed base. The CFO said ASML is "aggressively developing more upgrades" so customers obtain immediate capacity; the 2026 IBM growth outlook moved from more than 30% to about 50%.
  • Pricing. Value-based pricing negotiations with key customers are complete (partial benefit in 2027, full from 2028), covering critical products, associated upgrades and services; ASML does not intend to repeat the exercise annually.
  • Ecosystem rather than M&A. The 12-inch mask initiative, YieldStar 550 (more than 60% productivity improvement), the Brainport campus (first phase 2029) and supplier co-investment.
  • Resourcing. Non-current contract liabilities rose by about 5 to 6 billion EUR in Q3; cash was 12.7 billion EUR; R&D runs at about 1.2 billion EUR a quarter. The gap is quantification, which management has deferred to the January 2027 results and the CMD of 10 June 2027.

For the model: the strategy supports revenue growth exceeding unit growth (price, mix, upgrades) from 2027; the 2028 unit number is the key undisclosed input.

  • FY2026 revenue. 34 to 39 billion EUR (January) to 36 to 40 (April) to 43 to 45 (July) to 44 to 45 (October): the midpoint rose by about 8 billion EUR, or about 22%.

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5529th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: entry into new markets is modest, adjacent and sensible; it widens the holistic-lithography moat and adds options rather than near-term revenue. None of it is a material modelling input before 2028.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

A822nd of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: price rises are real and above cost inflation, and the last six months converted a latent value-based pricing opportunity into a completed commercial action. The 2026 benefit is small; the benefit is partial in 2027 and full from 2028. The magnitude is undisclosed, which is the key modelling uncertainty.

  • Completed action. At Q3 the CFO said value-based pricing negotiations with key customers were complete, with increases on critical high-volume products and associated upgrades and services, framed as compensation for productivity, yield and accuracy; "most" of the benefit should reach gross margin after capacity-ramp costs. He said ASML would not renegotiate annually and would stay within the value-based framework even if the industry weakens.
  • Evidence of real increases. EUV ASPs have risen about 5% a year with minimal resistance; NXE:3800 pricing rose 5 to 8% on optics upgrades; expert calls cite 5 to 7% annual increases with memory customers and 5 to 8% on High-NA with zero discounting; a semiconductor executive reports system pricing up 3 to 6% while ASML pushes suppliers for cost reductions. ASPs held through the 2023 to 2024 downturn with GM at 51.3% in both years on the company basis.
  • New-model pricing. Bernstein projects EUV ASPs up about 10% in 2027 and 7% in 2028, and the 3800E to 3800F transition carrying about a 20% price increase over two years (for about 13% more throughput). A 1,000-watt source enabling 330 wafers an hour could take Low-NA ASPs toward 350 million EUR (Bernstein).
  • Services. The Elec reported a 10% increase in South Korean replacement-parts prices from January 2027, reportedly accepted by Samsung and SK hynix (not confirmed by ASML).
  • Ceiling. TSMC's and Samsung's deferral of High-NA (about 380 million US dollars a tool) shows where value pricing meets multi-patterning as a cheaper substitute; customers impose cost-of-ownership limits.

For the model: treat like-for-like Low-NA price as rising mid-single digits in 2027 and high-single digits in 2028 including the value-based reset, plus mix from the 3800F; flow most but not all to GM.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5028th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no M&A step-up; the only transaction of note is the 2025 minority investment in Mistral AI. Capital goes to capacity, suppliers and buybacks. Neutral for the case.

  • Mistral AI: 1.3 billion EUR for about 11% (September 2025), about 0.25% of today's market value; CFO on the strategic committee; used for tool diagnostics; revalued in Q3 2026 with a 509.7 million EUR gain. It is defensible strategically but carries no modelled profit case and adds non-operating noise to EPS.
  • Supply-chain capital takes the place of M&A: the 24.9% Zeiss SMT stake (2017), interest-free loans to Zeiss (1,647 million EUR non-current loans receivable; maximum exposure about 3.9 billion EUR including advances and equity), Cymer (2013), Berliner Glas.
  • No acquisition, disposal, joint-venture restructuring or activist intervention was disclosed in the Q3 materials.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

A745th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns have stepped up sharply in 2026, faster than the announced programme implies, funded by customers' down payments as much as by earnings; the pace makes a larger or new programme, or a slowdown in 2027, a near-term decision. The balance sheet stays net cash, so there is no leverage use.

  • Buyback pace. A 12 billion EUR programme was announced for 2026 to 2028 (about 4 billion EUR a year). Treasury-share purchases were 6,148 million EUR in the first nine months of 2026 (FactSet shows 6,040 million year to date), including about 4.1 billion EUR (about 2.8 million shares) in Q3 alone. Half the three-year programme has been spent in nine months. In FY2025 buybacks were 5,808 million EUR.
  • Dividend. FY2025 dividend 7.50 EUR (+17%); quarterly interim dividends of 1.88 EUR in 2026; consensus DPS 8.62, 10.21, 13.13 EUR for FY2026 to FY2028 (a payout of only about 17 to 20% of consensus EPS).
  • Balance sheet. Net cash 10,772 million EUR at Q3 2026 (cash 12,735; debt 1,963); Q3 FCF 11.4 billion EUR. But contract liabilities of perhaps 24.6 billion EUR (Bernstein's estimate) mean much of the cash is customers' money against future deliveries.
  • Tension. The stronger 2028 capacity ambition requires investment in module-build, suppliers, Brainport and people; management says significant cash will continue to be returned but did not quantify the trade-off.
  • Share count. FactSet shares outstanding fell from 419.8 million (FY2019) to 385.4 million (FY2025); 382.8 million diluted now. Consensus implies about 376 million by FY2028 (net income 28,767 million over EPS 76.44).

For the model: at the current pace the 12 billion EUR programme completes around mid-2027; model either an upsized programme (likely, given FCF) or a share-count decline of about 1.5 to 2.5% a year, not the 0.7% a year in the prior memo.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–62

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four further positive internal forces are present: the conversion of the installed base into a higher-growth, software-rich annuity; a retention programme sized for the ramp; productivity-led product cadence; and a more disciplined demand-acceptance process. Each makes the earnings path more durable than a pure unit cycle.

  • Installed base as a growth engine. IBM revenue rose from 2,134 million EUR (Q4 2025) to 3,289 million (Q3 2026), with about 3.7 billion EUR guided for Q4. Bernstein expects IBM to compound at about 23% to roughly 23 billion EUR by 2030; Morgan Stanley argues software-rich upgrades for 3600D and 3800E systems still have runway because the 3800E upgrade cycle is early. The installed base never shrinks, so service revenue grows with the fleet.
  • Retention. A one-time conditional share award of 20,000 EUR per eligible employee, granted 1 January 2027 and vesting 1 January 2030 (about 900 million EUR in aggregate), covers exactly the ramp years. Attrition was 3.9% in 2025.
  • Productivity cadence. NXE:3800F (260 wafers an hour) in small numbers in 2027 and larger from 2028; NXE:4000F and 4200G behind it; immersion at 330 wafers an hour; YieldStar 550. ASML sells productivity, which supports pricing.
  • Order discipline. Every accepted purchase order requires a meaningful down payment and carries an exit cost; requests are checked against fab construction, clean-room availability and release schedules; not all 2029 to 2030 requests have been accepted. This reduces (does not remove) double-ordering risk.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5017th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there are no controlling actors to indicate change: free float is about 98%, no activist holds more than 3%, and short interest is about 0.15% of free float. The only structural actor is the preference-share foundation, whose role is defensive. Neutral.

  • Stichting Preferente Aandelen ASML holds an option over cumulative preference shares that could halve ordinary voting power against a hostile bid; under the Dutch structure regime the independent Supervisory Board holds key appointment powers.
  • The shareholders' association VEB questioned short-term incentive transparency at the April 2026 AGM, and shareholders approved up to 170,000 shares for the Board of Management incentive programme; neither is a push for strategic change.
  • Customers are the de facto powerful actors (four customers were 61.2% of FY2025 revenue; one legacy customer still holds co-investment shares), but they act through orders and pricing negotiations, not governance.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B+765th of 49

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are strong, broad and unusually well resourced, because customers are financing them; their limitation is that they are responses to external demand rather than a self-generated transformation, and their payoff in 2028 depends on supply-chain execution that management has not yet quantified.

  • Strong. Guidance raised three times (about +22% at the midpoint); a capacity decision (30% for 2027, more than 30% under investigation for 2028); completed value-based pricing; the installed base turned into a capacity product (IBM about +50% in 2026); buybacks running at roughly twice the programme pace.
  • Broad. The changes span volume (capacity, throughput), price (value-based resets, parts), mix (EUV share, software upgrades, metrology), cost (delayering, cycle time) and capital return. They touch every line from revenue to share count.
  • Resourced. Non-refundable down payments (non-current CL 9.3 billion EUR at Q3; total CL perhaps 24.6 billion EUR) fund the build; net cash 10.8 billion EUR; R&D about 4.8 billion EUR a year; a retention grant for the ramp years.
  • Limits. 2027 units are fixed; the 2028 increment depends on module-build, specialist suppliers and people; the CMD (June 2027) is the first point at which the ambition becomes measurable. The pattern to remember: forces of this kind (company response to an external surge) have historically been followed at ASML by digestion years, so they strengthen the 2026 to 2028 path more than they prove 2029.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

A623rd of 49

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: there is a well-founded, dated staircase whose central step is the 10 June 2027 CMD; its novelty is moderate (the market expects higher targets and a 2028 capacity increase) but its magnitude is genuinely unknown (units, price-reset size, margin path), which is where positive surprise can come from. The near-term steps are mixed, with two policy dates on the negative side.

Within the next six months (to April 2027):

  • 27 January 2027 results. FY2026 out-turn (about 44.5 billion EUR; GM about 56%), 2027 guidance (revenue, GM, IBM), the annual backlog (the first company figure since 38.8 billion EUR; Bernstein infers about 56 billion EUR), and possibly a 2028 capacity number. Novelty: moderate; foundation: strong.
  • Capital return. At the current pace the 12 billion EUR buyback is half spent; an enlarged or new programme is likely within 6 to 12 months. Novelty: moderate; foundation: strong (Q3 FCF 11.4 billion EUR; net cash 10.8 billion EUR).
  • Pricing visible in practice. Korean spare-parts price rise from January 2027 (reported); first partial value-based pricing in 2027 orders and shipments. Novelty: high, because the size is undisclosed.
  • Customer capex. TSMC Q4 and 2027 capex guidance (January); memory makers' 2027 capex. Foundation: strong.
  • Negative-side dates. 10 November 2026 (expiry of the US Affiliates Rule suspension and China's rare-earth suspension); year-end US defence bill (MATCH Act vehicle); monthly memory price data around the expected price-growth peak.

Months six to eighteen (to April 2028):

  • 10 June 2027 CMD. New 2030 targets (consensus FY2028 already about 23% above the old 2030 ceiling), 2028 units, a margin path toward 60%, IBM ambition. Novelty: moderate in direction, high in magnitude; foundation: strong.
  • Value-based pricing in reported margins (partial 2027); delayering savings from May 2027; first NXE:3800F shipments; the 85-unit Low-NA delivery year; High-NA orders for Samsung's and SK hynix's 2028 DRAM insertion; YieldStar 550 ramp.
  • Tests that could disappoint. Q1 2027 IBM (a sequential fall of more than 20% is the earnings review's threshold); Q4 2026 China system sales (above 2.5 billion EUR is the threshold); TSMC capex (a cut of more than 10% is the alarm).

Months eighteen to thirty-six (to late 2029):

  • 2028 capacity above 110 Low-NA systems; the full value-based pricing year; High-NA in volume DRAM manufacturing (2028) and the run-up to TSMC's A10 (about 2030); Brainport campus phase one (2029); possible 2028 orders for 2029 to 2030 converting. Foundation: medium; this is where the AI-capex and memory cycle determine the outcome, and where the digestion risk sits.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

A901st of 49

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

A901st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry and value chain support the thesis about as strongly as a structure can: a monopoly in the gating tool, inside a margin-first oligopoly, sold directly to customers who pre-pay, with share drifting toward the leader and prices that held through the last downturn. The fragilities are specific and known: policy (China shipments and servicing, with a reinforcing loop between domestic substitution and export bans), supplier scalability (now the cap on 2028 units) and customer concentration (four customers about 61% of revenue; Taiwan and Korea about two-thirds of system sales). The historical pattern for the sector says the share price de-rates before the earnings peak; ASML has done so while visibility has lengthened, so the precedent cuts both ways. For the model: hold share and pricing assumptions firm through 2028, put the policy risk into an explicit China scenario, and put supply into the 2028 unit range rather than into the base-case margin.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?A851st of 49full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

A861st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is direct, with no intermediaries in the sale or service; the only intermediaries are the banks that finance customers' down payments, which help ASML (they accelerate cash) and introduce a small credit-conditions fragility.

  • ASML sells and services directly under contracts of up to five years, with volume purchase agreements; revenue recognition under these agreements is PwC's sole key audit matter. Customer satisfaction was 88% in 2025 (86% in 2024).
  • Customers use bank factoring to pay required down payments (6.2 billion EUR of receivables derecognised in FY2025; days sales outstanding about 34 against Applied's 73). If credit tightened, customers would carry more of the financing themselves; this would raise their cost, not ASML's, and could slow marginal orders.
  • Site acceptance testing at customer fabs governs revenue timing for new products, so customer readiness rather than any channel is the timing variable.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

A801st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving rationally and are confined to segments that matter little to ASML's earnings; their behaviour neither helps nor materially hurts, and ASML's position against them is robust at the leading edge and only mildly exposed in mature DUV and packaging lithography.

  • Canon: KrF, i-line, packaging steppers, nanoimprint in evaluation; strong AI-related packaging demand and memory-related KrF orders; Industrial Group growth target of at least 10% a year.
  • Nikon: 29 units planned for fiscal 2027; DSP-110 digital lithography for large packaging substrates (April 2027) and a 0.5-micrometre system in development with Japanese government support; mask-compatible immersion second source later in the decade.
  • Indirect competitors: Lam and Tokyo Electron patterning solutions reduce lithography steps at the margin; packaging capex accrues to deposition, etch and bonding.
  • Fragility: low. The peripheral share at risk affects ASML's adjacency growth options (XT:260 packaging) more than its core earnings.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F2845th of 49full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

A701st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption forces are acting on almost every actor, and most of them, net, strengthen ASML's position; the exceptions are the policy actors (where a domestic Chinese tool lowers the political cost of a ban) and the financing of demand (where AI-capex funding stress could alter the timing of 2028 to 2029 orders).

  • Demand. AI accelerators and HBM re-architect demand toward more layers per wafer (NVIDIA's next architectures need up to ten times more silicon within three years); token-price deflation and open-weight models could reduce compute per task, weakening the link between AI usage and WFE. Net positive, with a financing question.
  • Competition. Chinese state capital disrupts the mature DUV order inside China; Huawei's umbrella gives domestic tools adoption clout. Negative at the edge.
  • Regulators and politicians. A domestic Chinese tool lowers Washington's political cost of a ban, but (per Bernstein) may also reduce the policy effectiveness of mature-node bans and so the argument for them. Two-sided, net negative.
  • Suppliers. Zeiss High-NA mirror capacity is gated by metrology-chamber test cycles; a free-electron laser would disrupt TRUMPF's role rather than ASML's; specialist suppliers need investment to scale. Neutral to slightly negative for unit upside.
  • Customers' operations. Cleanroom, power and labour bottlenecks delay readiness and lift demand for productivity upgrades in the meantime. Timing negative, margin positive.
  • ASML itself. Software upgrades and higher-throughput tools disrupt its own unit model, shifting value from units to productivity.

Fragility: low for EUV economics; moderate for China DUV and IBM; moderate for 2028 to 2029 order timing.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

A961st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: ASML is number one by a wide margin in lithography (about 90% of revenue; 100% of EUV; about 85 to 92% of DUV) and one of the two largest WFE vendors overall; no other company is within reach in its activity.

  • Lithography volume shares: Canon about 5%, Nikon about 1%. ASML retained about 92% of DUV in FY2024 (Bernstein; an expert call estimates 85%).
  • In total front-end WFE, ASML (about 22%) and Applied Materials (about 17%) lead, followed by Lam (13%), Tokyo Electron (10%) and KLA (8%) for CY2025. One research line placing Applied ahead is inconsistent with its own figures and should be ignored.
