C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A961st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the market is about as concentrated as any in the investable universe; both tests pass by a wide margin.

  • WFE: the top five hold more than 70% of front-end equipment revenue.
  • Lithography: the distribution is a monopoly with a fringe (ASML about 90%, Canon about 5%, Nikon about 1%); the leader holds many times the share of numbers two to ten combined, far above the five-times test.
  • Adjacent concentration is similar (Advantest 63% of test; KLA 57% of process control; VAT about 70 to 76% of vacuum valves), so ASML's suppliers and peers also operate in tight structures, which reinforces price discipline across the chain.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

A881st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares are very stable to rising at the leading edge; new entrants have taken no share outside China, and inside China their gains in lithography are still prospective. Stability is high over the model horizon; the one erosion vector is state-directed substitution in Chinese mature-node DUV.

  • EUV has been 100% ASML since commercialisation; Nikon's lithography share has dwindled to about 1%; the Netherlands supplied a record 89% of China's lithography imports in 2024 even under export restrictions.
  • Chinese lithography self-sufficiency is below 1% (CCB International); domestic equipment content across all process steps in mature Chinese fabs is 20 to 40%, with substitution fastest in etch, deposition and cleaning, not lithography. China's overall WFE self-sufficiency was about 21% in 2025.
  • Entrant plans: Shanghai Aishengna about five immersion tools in 2026 and 20 in 2027 (yields 10 to 20%); experts put SMEE 15 to 20 years behind in advanced lithography.

For the model: hold EUV share at 100% and global DUV share broadly stable to 2028, with a China-only erosion of perhaps a few points of China DUV volume from 2027.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

A901st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share consolidates strongly toward ASML, which is both the largest and the most advantaged competitor; the mechanism is structural (each node raises R&D cost, optical complexity and customer co-dependence), and only Chinese state policy runs against it.

  • The 2012 customer co-investment programme (Intel, TSMC, Samsung funded 1.4 billion EUR of EUV engineering) tied the three largest chipmakers' roadmaps to ASML's cadence.
  • The advantage is systemic (more than 100,000 components, more than 40 subsystems, about 900 product suppliers integrated over decades; Zeiss optics behind a 24.9% stake), not merely scale, so a well-funded rival cannot buy its way in on a three- to five-year view.
  • Holistic lithography (scanner, computational lithography, YieldStar metrology, e-beam inspection) feeds back into the scanner and raises switching costs each cycle.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

A881st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the economic competitors put margin first and behave benignly; ASML itself sets the industry's price discipline. Prices did not fall when volume fell in 2023 to 2024. The only competitors willing to accept losses are state-backed Chinese entrants pursuing self-sufficiency, confined to China's mature nodes.

  • ASML refuses hardware discounts on EUV, using multi-tool packages, service bundling and installation priority as the only incentives; ASPs held through downturns when customers were loss-making; company-basis GM was 51.3% in both FY2023 and FY2024.
  • WFE peers keep capacity discipline over share wars (Jefferies). Nikon's precision segment is loss-making (operating loss of 112.4 billion yen in fiscal 2026) and is pursuing a service-led and second-source recovery rather than a price war. Canon competes in mature and packaging niches.
  • Chinese entrants (Aishengna, SMEE, Huawei-linked) do not price for return; this is the one actor a monopoly cannot price against, but it acts only inside China.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

A821st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the attitude is stable among economic actors and there is no shark-attack risk at the leading edge, because no competitor can reach it; the change over three years is two adverse threads at the edge (Chinese state-backed entry, Nikon's second-source pitch) and one favourable thread (customers engaging in, rather than resisting, value-based pricing).

  • Canon and Nikon cannot enter EUV; yen weakness lets them price mature DUV aggressively, which is pressure, not a shark attack. Nikon's mask-compatible immersion platform (prototype 2028, ramp fiscal 2031) is aimed at customers tired of rising tool prices, a medium-term check on DUV pricing.
  • Chinese entrants are unpredictable by objective (self-sufficiency, not return), so their behaviour is situation-dependent and policy-driven; their reach is limited by yields and throughput.
  • Customers' attitude has shifted favourably: constructive pricing discussions picked up through 2026 and concluded at Q3; the ceiling is visible at High-NA, where TSMC and Samsung deferred adoption on cost.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

A881st of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure is as supportive as any in the universe (a monopoly inside a margin-first oligopoly, with share drifting to the leader); its support is robust to cyclical weakness and fragile only to policy, specifically Chinese state-backed substitution combined with export-control escalation, which together could erode the China DUV franchise over three to five years.

  • Supportive: concentration, stable shares, consolidation toward the most advantaged player, margin-first behaviour, prices that hold through downturns.
  • Fragility mapped: (i) China policy loop (domestic tools lower the political cost of bans; bans accelerate domestic qualification); (ii) a mature-node second source (Nikon, from about 2029 to 2031); (iii) customers' pushback where a cheaper process route exists (multi-patterning versus High-NA). None of these touches EUV economics within the model horizon.
  • Cyclicality is not a structural fragility here: in the 2023 to 2024 digestion, prices and margins held while volume stalled.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?A821st of 49full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C5815th of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a moderate and slowly rising threat to the incremental intensity story and to High-NA timing, not to EUV demand within four years; the most relevant substitute in the model window is internal (software upgrades and tool reuse substituting for some new tools).

  • Multi-patterning with Low-NA instead of High-NA. TSMC's chosen route for A14; it delays High-NA revenue but increases Low-NA demand (more exposures).
  • Advanced packaging and hybrid bonding instead of shrink. Shifts capital toward deposition, etch and bonding (Lam packaging revenue +70%; BESI hybrid bonding); lithography still participates through substrates and interconnect formation.
  • Materials and process engineering. GAA shifts critical-dimension control toward deposition and etch; dry resist and gas-cluster etch reduce exposure doses and steps.
  • 3D DRAM. Could limit EUV layers in DRAM around 2030 or later; delays to 2032 would extend lateral lithography intensity.
  • Upgrades and reuse. Customers pursue tool reuse across generations and buy throughput upgrades; this substitutes some new-tool purchases with higher-margin ASML revenue, so it is a mix and timing effect.
  • Trend. Slowly rising, mainly beyond 2028.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

A921st of 49

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

A931st of 498 answers
Judgement93C-4194C-4293C-4382C-4492C-4594C-4658C-4790
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A931st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

ASML's competitive advantage is the strongest in the universe and still widening: a physics-, capital- and partnership-based monopoly in EUV, a dominant DUV position, an installed base that compounds high-margin revenue, and demonstrated, applied pricing power that has just been reset upward. The advantage strengthens mechanically with each node. Its limits are not competitive but structural: pricing meets a substitute at High-NA, sharing is partial, policy can remove China, and supply caps how fast the advantage can be monetised. For the model: assume no erosion of EUV share or pricing through 2029; let price contribute mid- to high-single digits a year from 2027 to 2028; and treat the installed base as a structural grower with a cyclical software component.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A941st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: ASML holds six mutually reinforcing moats, four of them very strong and widening at the leading edge; the only narrowing is at the mature DUV edge inside China, driven by policy rather than competition.

  • Production and technology (very strong, widening). 100% of EUV; more than 20 years of development; High-NA now in production at Intel with availability near 90%; NXE:3800F and 4000F, 1,000-watt source; the 12-inch mask initiative positions ASML to shape High-NA's infrastructure. R&D of about 4.7 billion EUR a year (14.4% of sales) raises the bar each generation.
  • Upstream optical lock (very strong, stable). Zeiss SMT as sole supplier, 24.9% owned and financed; Cymer (light source, owned since 2013); TRUMPF exclusive lasers.
  • System integration (very strong, stable). More than 100,000 components, more than 40 subsystems, about 900 product suppliers integrated over decades; 95 to 97% uptime against under 70% for Chinese tools.
  • Customer switching costs and co-dependence (very strong, widening). Process re-qualification risk, proprietary computational lithography (OPC), trained engineers; co-development of nodes with TSMC; holistic lithography (YieldStar, e-beam) adds a metrology lock; an installed base that grows service and upgrade revenue.
  • Scale (strong, widening). The largest WFE revenue base in a narrow product line; ability to pre-fund suppliers and win scarce European cleanroom space.
  • Regulatory or political (two-sided). Export controls exclude Chinese tools from the rest of the world and exclude ASML from China's leading edge; the Dutch state's licensing is both a moat and a constraint.
  • Culture and knowledge (present). Attrition about 4%; knowledge concentrated in veteran Dutch engineers (a concentration as well as a moat).
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A931st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: ASML's scale is the largest in its market by an order of magnitude at the leading edge and among the largest in WFE overall, and it confers three things competitors cannot match: the R&D budget to stay generations ahead, the balance sheet to finance its supply chain, and the pricing leverage that comes from being the only source of the bottleneck tool.

  • Revenue 32.7 billion EUR (FY2025) rising to about 44.5 billion EUR (FY2026); Applied about 28 billion US dollars, Lam about 18 billion; Canon and Nikon lithography a small fraction. Headcount above 44,500 FTE; customer-support hiring of 1,000 in Taiwan.
  • R&D about 4.8 billion EUR a year on my Q3 run-rate, more than many competitors' total revenue in lithography.
  • Supply-chain finance: interest-free loans and advances to Zeiss (non-current loans receivable 1,647 million EUR; advances 1,192 million EUR in 2025); frame contracts with fixed delivery schedules for suppliers.
  • What scale does not confer: unit flexibility (the supply chain caps output) and immunity from customer concentration.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

A921st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: surplus customer utility is the highest in the semiconductor equipment chain and demonstrably leading: there is no alternative at the leading edge, and even in DUV the customers' own choices disprove inferiority. The ceiling appears only where a cheaper process route exists (High-NA versus multi-patterning).

  • No alternative at EUV. Advanced logic below 7nm and advanced DRAM cannot be produced at scale without it.
  • Non-inferiority disproved in DUV. The Netherlands' 89% share of China's lithography imports in 2024 even under restriction; Chinese domestic tools at 10 to 20% yields and 10 to 30 wafers an hour against about 200.
  • Productivity value. NXE:3800F 260 wafers an hour versus 230; the E-to-F transition gives customers about 45% more wafer capacity on about 30% more units; software upgrades that add capacity faster and cheaper than a new cleanroom sell at about 100% GM; tool uptime 95 to 97%; tools run efficiently for more than 20 years with upgrades.
  • Ceiling. TSMC skipping High-NA for A14 on cost (about 380 million US dollars a tool), and customers imposing cost-of-ownership limits (an expert from a sensor supplier).
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

A941st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: ASML has and uses pricing power, demonstrated by application: real price increases of about 5% a year on EUV for years, zero discounting, ASPs held through customers' loss-making downturns, and now a completed value-based price reset across critical products, upgrades and services. Relative utility has not eroded because pricing is tied to productivity. Management deliberately limits the frequency of resets, which caps the upside in any single year.

  • Applied evidence: EUV ASPs rising almost linearly at about 1 million EUR per wafer-an-hour of throughput gained; NXE:3800 up 5 to 8%; GM held at 51.3% through FY2023 to FY2024 (company basis); 2026 value-based negotiations completed (partial 2027, full 2028); reported 10% increase in Korean spare-parts prices from January 2027.
  • Erosion test: customers accepted increases while placing more orders and pre-paying; satisfaction rose to 88%.
  • Limits: the CFO will not renegotiate annually; pricing on High-NA meets a cheaper substitute; capacity-ramp costs absorb part of the gain; China DUV pricing faces local competition.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B5810th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: ASML shares scale economies de facto, through productivity rather than price cuts: each tool generation lowers the customer's cost per exposure even as ASP rises, and management frames value-based pricing as capturing "a fair share" of the productivity it delivers. Sharing is real but deliberately partial.

  • Cost per exposure falls as throughput rises faster than price (Bernstein: High-NA cost per exposure down about 23% through 2030 on throughput rising from 110 to 175 wafers an hour).
  • The E-to-F transition gives customers about 45% more wafer capacity on about 30% more units.
  • Management's stated principle (AGM April 2026): price in line with the value delivered, not to exploit temporary shortages; the CFO said ASML would stay within that framework even if the industry weakens.
  • The constraint on sharing more: ASML's own capacity build and supplier investment.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A901st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: mechanical forces dominate: lithography intensity rises with each DRAM and logic node, every installed tool adds a stream of service and upgrade revenue at higher margin, each generation raises the R&D bar for any entrant, and the largest customers' roadmaps are tied to ASML's cadence. The advantage and the financial outputs improve each period without requiring heroic execution.

  • Intensity: DRAM EUV exposures three (1b) to five or six (1c) to eight (1d); logic about 20 (N3), 22 (N2), 25 to 30 by A10.
  • Installed base: service fees of about 5% of EUV ASP a year; lifetime service worth up to 130% of tool value; IBM about 12 billion EUR in 2026.
  • Margin ratchet: GM 45.0% (2017) to 52.8% (2025, company basis) to about 56% (2026) on mix.
  • Entry bar: R&D about 4.7 billion EUR a year and rising; holistic lithography raises switching costs each cycle.
  • Peers: ASM International has a similar mechanism (ALD layers per node) but faces bundling; BESI's depends on a memory adoption date; AIXTRON's on a single demand vector. ASML's mechanism is the broadest and least contested.

Company - Management

A801st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is top-quartile among global semiconductor-equipment operators: an execution-focused chief executive with a strong, stable team, an engineering culture with low attrition, outlier operating standards, restrained pay and strong alignment, and a clear capital-allocation order. The two qualifications for the model are communication and calibration. Communication is deliberately incomplete on the variables that decide 2027 to 2028 (bookings, units, pricing size, margin), so forecasts must be built from inference until January 2027 and June 2027. Calibration has been conservative recently (repeated raises) but includes two externally driven cuts in four years; in a supply-constrained ramp, the 2022 precedent (supply-driven cut) is the relevant risk. Management quality supports giving the base case substantial weight; it does not insulate the case from policy or the cycle.

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

A862nd of 49

Judgement: the ambition is large and has grown in 2026: to roughly double Low-NA EUV and immersion capacity between 2026 and 2028 and then go further, to reset pricing to value, to make the installed base a growth engine, and to shape High-NA's industrial ecosystem. Relative to peers it is the most ambitious in scale; its formal long-range targets lag its rhetoric.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

A841st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the strategy is smart and moat-widening (holistic lithography, value-based pricing, productivity-led product cadence, supplier co-investment), bold on growth, and reasonably dynamic because capacity is added inside existing cleanrooms, in phases, and on customers' money. Its weakness is that it is optimised for a supply-limited world; in a demand pause, the capacity and supplier commitments become a cost.

  • Moat widening. Scanner plus computational lithography plus YieldStar plus e-beam raises switching costs; value-based pricing captures productivity; the Zeiss framework and frame contracts secure supply; the 12-inch mask initiative embeds ASML in High-NA's infrastructure.
  • Growth priority. Output is one of the chief executive's two daily priorities; demand is checked against fab plans before capacity is allocated.
  • Dynamism. Multiple output levers (factory space, cycle time, suppliers, throughput upgrades, installed-base upgrades) let ASML add customer wafer capacity without adding tools one-for-one; upgrades double as a hedge while cleanrooms lag; capacity growth is demand-led and phased with customer fab completions (Morgan Stanley).
  • Limits. Pricing resets are deliberately infrequent; the strategy does not yet specify how it would behave if 2029 orders soften after the 2028 build.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

B628th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the evidence shows shareholders are put ahead of management on pay, alignment and capital return, and governance is prudent in its scaffolding; the deviations from shareholder primacy are structural Dutch features (a preference-share defence, no director equity, a two-tier board) and a disclosure choice (no quarterly bookings) rather than self-serving behaviour.

  • Pay restraint. CEO total remuneration 7.02 million EUR (2025), less than half of Applied's and KLA's chief executives; total executive board pay below 0.3% of EBIT against a 2.1% sector base rate; cash bonus capped at 150% of salary; clawback policies (SEC and Dutch Civil Code).
  • Alignment. Variable pay about 80% of CEO target pay; LTI 35% relative TSR (against the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index) and 35% ROAIC with a 65% maximum-vesting hurdle; executives above shareholding guidelines; zero open-market insider selling in twelve months (heavy selling at Applied, Lam and KLA).
  • Capital return. 8.5 billion EUR returned in 2025; buybacks running at roughly twice the programme pace in 2026; a growing dividend.
  • Scaffolding. Fully independent nine-member Supervisory Board (44% women; average tenure 4.1 years); five committees; clean PwC first-year audit (54,000 hours; one key audit matter); Disclosure Committee and whistleblowing framework.
  • Deviations. Stichting Preferente Aandelen can halve ordinary voting power against a hostile bid; supervisory directors hold no equity (Dutch rules; three bought small stakes); VEB challenged STI transparency at the 2026 AGM; ROAIC definitions exclude some working-capital items; bookings disclosure withdrawn.
  • Why. The Dutch two-tier model and a culture of engineering stewardship produce restraint on pay and long-term orientation, while protecting the company against takeover and short-term pressure.

ASML's economic strength is outstanding on every output test (growth, margin, returns, balance sheet) and its weakness is shape rather than level: cash is lumpy and customer-funded, the 2026 margin jump contains a cyclical software component, and the operating leverage has not been tested by a revenue decline in this cycle. How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

The fair multiple therefore cannot be argued from a reinvestment runway; it must be argued from the duration of growth and the monopoly, with surplus cash returned.

  • Deployment avenues. Capacity (Low-NA and immersion +30% a year within existing footprint; Brainport campus first phase 2029); module-build expansion (Wilton, Taiwan); supplier capacity (Zeiss loans and advances; frame contracts); R&D (EXE, NXE:3800F, 4200G, metrology, e-beam, 3D integration); people (retention award about 900 million EUR).

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

A951st of 49

Judgement: returns are among the highest in global capital goods and rising: post-tax ROIC of 39% (FY2025), ROCE of 57%, and an implied return on tangible capital far above 30%.   • ROIC rose from 19.0% (2017) to 39.0% (2025) as capital intensity fell and margins rose; with FY2026 EBIT up about 64% and capital broadly stable, FY2026 ROIC will rise further.

Returns would fall if down payments shrank relative to inventory, which is what would happen in an order deferral.

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: margins are high and rising fast: GM about 56% in FY2026 (57.5% in Q3) from 51.6% in FY2025 (FactSet basis) and 43.4% in FY2019; EBIT margin about 42% in FY2026 from 34.6%. The trend is up through 2028 on consensus (GM about 57.6 to 57.9%; EBIT 45 to 47%), but the composition of the 2026 jump (software upgrades) makes the 2027 to 2028 path flatter and more contested than the headline suggests.

  • History (FactSet basis). GM 43.4%, 47.4%, 51.9%, 49.5%, 50.2%, 49.8%, 51.6% (FY2019 to FY2025); EBIT margin 23.6%, 29.0%, 35.1%, 30.7%, 32.8%, 31.9%, 34.6%. The company basis shows 52.8% GM for FY2025.
  • 2026 quarterly. GM 53.0%, 54.0%, 57.5%, about 57% guided; EBIT margin 36.0%, 37.1%, 44.4%.
  • What drove 2026. Higher volume and absorption, EUV and immersion mix, and very high-margin installed-base upgrades. Jefferies estimates productivity-upgrade software contributed nearly 400 basis points of the 2026 GM expansion (about 200 basis points at Q2).
  • The 2027 to 2028 tug-of-war. Up: value-based pricing (partial 2027, full 2028), EUV mix, NXE:3800F mix, the 60% drop-through. Down: normalising upgrade demand as customer cleanrooms come on line, High-NA at about 20% GM early, capacity-ramp and supplier costs, NXE:3800F launch costs, prolonged reliance on the 3800F rather than the 4200G. Broker views span Jefferies (58.3% in 2027, flat 58.2% in 2028), Deutsche Bank (60% by Q4 2028), Redburn (60% by FY2030), Bernstein (above 60%, reaching 61%, by 2030), against the company's 2030 framework of 56 to 60%.
  • Consensus operating leverage. Operating expenses (gross profit less EBIT) rise from about 6.2 billion EUR (FY2026) to 7.4 and 7.8 billion EUR, falling from about 14% of revenue to 10.5% by FY2028; most of the EBIT margin gain in FY2028 is opex leverage, not GM.

For the model: a GM of about 56% in 2026, 57 to 58% in 2027 and 57 to 59% in 2028 is defensible; above 59% in 2028 requires the full pricing benefit with limited upgrade normalisation.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

A852nd of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is clean at the line that matters (brokers' adjusted EPS equals reported basic EPS in FY2025), with three items to adjust: the 2026 Mistral revaluation gain (non-operating, about 3% of FY2026 EPS), the cyclical software-upgrade component of margin (real cash, but not a run-rate), and the IFRS treatment of capitalised development (which depresses the statutory margin, not the US GAAP figures used by the market). The tax rate is low and the leakage risk is upward.

  • One-offs. Q3 2026 net income of 4,960 million EUR includes a 509.7 million EUR gain from revaluing the Mistral stake; adjusted Q3 EPS about 11.6 EUR against 12.95 reported. Consensus FY2026 median EPS (42.49 EUR) appears to include it; the mean (41.19 EUR) appears not to.
  • Accounting basis. ASML reports under US GAAP for the market and IFRS statutorily. IFRS capitalises development costs and amortises them: H1 2026 IFRS GM was 50.9% against 53.0% and 54.0% on the US GAAP quarterly figures; US GAAP net income was 5,674 million EUR against IFRS 5,236 million in H1 2026.
  • Margin quality. Software upgrades at about 100% GM are genuine revenue and cash, but their demand depends on customers' capacity shortage; treat a part of the 2026 margin as cyclical.
  • Tax. Reported tax rate 17.7% (FY2025) under the Dutch innovation box (6.9% in FY2019, 18.6% in FY2024); consensus implies about 18% in FY2027 and FY2028. A Pillar Two or Dutch innovation-box change is the leakage risk (the prior memo tested 21%).
  • Other. Receivables factoring flatters receivables, not earnings; the Zeiss interest-free loans are a hidden cost of supply (forgone interest on about 1.6 to 1.9 billion EUR); D&A will rise with capacity and capitalised development (consensus 1.0 to 1.3 billion EUR; the prior memo's adverse case used 1.9 billion EUR by FY2028).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

A881st of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is no financial leverage; the company is net cash (10.8 billion EUR at Q3 2026; net debt to EBITDA about -0.7x), so EPS is neither amplified nor endangered by gearing. The only leverage-like feature is customer funding through contract liabilities, which is non-refundable and operationally, not financially, risky.

  • Debt 1,963 million EUR (falling from 4,994 million at end-2024); cash and short-term investments 12,735 million EUR; net interest is a small income line (consensus pre-tax profit exceeds EBIT by about 0.4 to 0.9 billion EUR a year).
  • Hedging: euro billing keeps translation sensitivity tiny (a 10% move in foreign currencies changed 2025 net income by about 1.6 million EUR); forward hedges cover 12 months of forecast transactions.
  • Implication: buybacks (not leverage) are the EPS lever; a levered recapitalisation is neither mooted nor needed.

5a

Pricing power

3 answers

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A862nd of 49

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price is the second-largest driver of revenue growth after units in 2027 and 2028 and the largest single driver of gross-margin improvement. I estimate like-for-like price (excluding product mix) adds about 3.5 percentage points to FY2027 revenue growth and about 4 points to FY2028, which is about 1.6 billion EUR of extra revenue in FY2027 and about 3.8 billion EUR (cumulative against FY2026) in FY2028, at close to full margin; new-product mix (NXE:3800E to 3800F) adds a further 3 to 4 points to EUV average selling prices each year. About half of the gross pricing benefit to the margin rate is absorbed by capacity-ramp costs, High-NA dilution and the normalisation of upgrade mix, so the gross margin rises about 0.7 points a year rather than 1.5 to 2 points. Price will hold in a 2029 slowdown, because ASML has never cut price in a downturn; what falls in a slowdown is mix (upgrades) and volume, not price.

The evidence on size.

  • Completed value-based pricing (CFO, 14 October 2026). Negotiations complete with key customers on critical high-volume products, associated upgrades and services; partial benefit in 2027, full from 2028 as legacy backlog is delivered; "most" reaches gross margin after funding capacity; not to be repeated annually; ASML will stay within the framework even if the industry weakens. Size undisclosed.
  • Broker sizing. Bernstein: EUV ASP +10% (2027) and +7% (2028), 2028 GM 58.9%, 2028 EPS 84 EUR. Jefferies: GM 58.3% (2027) and a flat or lower 2028 (58.2%, or 55.2% if upgrades fall). Deutsche Bank: pricing partly absorbed by capacity cost; 60% GM by Q4 2028. Redburn: 60% by 2030.
  • Model and parts pricing. NXE:3800E blended ASP about 220 million EUR (CFO); NXE:3800F about 290 million EUR at 260 wafers an hour (Bernstein, April 2026), and elsewhere Bernstein frames the 3800E-to-3800F step as about +20% over two years; either way, +20 to +30% price for +13% throughput is stronger than the historical rule of about 1 million EUR per wafer-an-hour, which is the clearest sign that value-based pricing is already in the new-model price list; EXE:5200B about 360 million EUR against about 250 million for the EXE:5000. Korean replacement-parts prices +10% from January 2027, accepted by Samsung and SK hynix (The Elec, 12 October 2026; not confirmed by ASML; not yet evidenced in other regions).
  • History of holding price. GM 51.3% (company basis) in both FY2023 and FY2024 during digestion; Q4 2019 GM 48.1% in the 2019 memory correction; no hardware discounts on EUV. The one lapse was lagged cost pass-through: GM fell from 50.6% (2021) to 49.7% (2022) on supplier and labour inflation, recovered through inflation compensation negotiated in late 2023.

My price build (base case).

  • Low-NA EUV ASP: about 235 million EUR (FY2026 blended, my derivation), 254 (FY2027, +8%: about +3 to 4% like-for-like price, the rest NXE:3800E full-year mix and early 3800F), 274 (FY2028, +8%: full like-for-like pricing plus more 3800F), then +4% a year. Below Bernstein's +10% in 2027 because the CFO said the 2027 benefit is partial and 3800F ships in small quantities in 2027.
  • DUV: +2 to 3% a year like-for-like, less in China, where yen-priced Canon and Nikon tools and Shanghai Aishengna's state-backed immersion tools set a ceiling; NXT throughput upgrades sold at a premium.
  • IBM: +5 to 10% on parts and service rates from 2027 (Korea first); value-based pricing on upgrades. Service revenue growth of about 12 to 15% a year combines fleet growth and price.
  • High-NA: price holds near 360 to 400 million EUR, but the ceiling is visible: TSMC defers to about 2030 and Samsung logic to 2030 on cost of ownership; the 12-inch photomask initiative (pilot 2031, production 2033) is the route to raising the value delivered and therefore the price.

Contribution to revenue (my arithmetic on the base case). FY2027 revenue growth of 25.7% (+11.4 billion EUR) decomposes into about +16 points from volume (19 more Low-NA units at the 2026 ASP, +4.5 billion; DUV volume net of China normalisation, +2.7 billion), +3.6 points like-for-like price (about 1.6 billion: 0.8 EUV, 0.45 DUV, 0.35 IBM), +1.8 points EUV product mix, +0.6 points High-NA, and +3.6 points IBM volume (service fleet growth and upgrades). FY2028's 24.4% (+13.7 billion) is about +16 points volume (26 more Low-NA units, +6.6 billion; DUV +2.3 billion), +3.9 points like-for-like price (about 2.2 billion more), +1.8 points mix, +2.3 points High-NA and +0.5 points IBM volume. Price is therefore about one-seventh of revenue growth but about one-fifth (FY2027) to one-quarter (FY2028) of gross-profit growth.

Contribution to margin. Pricing worth about 1.6 billion EUR of revenue in FY2027 and 3.8 billion EUR in FY2028 at about 90% incremental margin would add roughly 1.3 points and 2.5 points of GM respectively against FY2026. Offsets: upgrade mix normalising (upgrades about 12% of revenue in 2026, 11.4% in 2027, 8.6% in 2028: about -1.3 points by 2028), High-NA (3 to 4.5% of revenue at 25 to 40% GM: -1.0 to -1.2 points, roughly unchanged from 2026), capacity-ramp and 3800F launch costs (-0.5 to -0.8 points in 2027). Net: 55.7% (FY2026) to 57.6% (FY2027) to 58.3% (FY2028).

Durability in the operating conditions that matter.

  • Supply-constrained up-cycle (2026 to 2028): strongest; customers pre-pay 35 to 40% and accept increases.
  • Digestion (my 2029): price holds (precedent 2019, 2023 to 2024); but the next value-based reset slips, and the services price rise is the only new price lever.
  • Memory price break: price holds; volume and upgrades fall.
  • China export controls: China DUV pricing is the weakest link; I assume flat China prices.
  • Strong euro: ASML bills in euros, so a strong euro raises customers' dollar cost without changing ASML's price; it slows marginal orders rather than prices.

What would change my view. Upward: the January 2027 GM guide above 58% or a disclosed price percentage at the June 2027 Capital Markets Day; evidence that the Korean parts increase is global. Downward: the 2027 GM guide at or below the Q4 2026 exit rate (about 57%) despite pricing, which would mean capacity cost absorbs more than half the benefit.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7564P-0190
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

B+649th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: operating leverage is genuine but, at today's high margins, moderate in ratio terms: incremental revenue drops through at about 55 to 60%, so a 10% change in revenue moves EBIT by roughly 12 to 14% at a 45% margin. The wider risk is mix: a fall in software upgrades (near-100% margin) moves EBIT almost one-for-one, and a revenue shortfall in a downturn would come with margin-dilutive mix. The resulting cone for FY2028 EBIT is roughly plus or minus 25% around consensus.

  • Evidence of drop-through. FY2026 versus FY2025: revenue +11.8 billion EUR, EBIT +7.2 billion EUR (about 61%), matching management's expected 60% drop-through by 2027. Q2 2026 EBIT +30% on revenue +21%. Consensus FY2027 and FY2028 drop-through is about 56% and 54%.
  • Fixed-cost base. Operating expenses about 6.4 billion EUR a year (R&D about 4.8 billion, SG&A about 1.6 billion); D&A about 1.1 billion EUR; a factory and field-service labour base sized for 30% annual growth.
  • History in weak years. Revenue never fell between FY2019 and FY2025; in flat years EBIT was flat (FY2024: revenue +2.6%, EBIT -0.2%) and in a year of supply disruption EBIT stalled despite growth (FY2022: revenue +13.8%, EBIT -0.5%, as GM fell to 49.5%). The model has not been tested by a revenue decline in this cycle.
  • Range (my arithmetic). FY2028 consensus: revenue 73.7 billion EUR, EBIT 34.6 billion EUR. A downside of about 62 billion EUR (about 100 Low-NA units, little pricing, IBM flat) at a 60 to 65% decremental takes EBIT to about 27 to 28 billion EUR (-20%); losing a further 1.5 billion EUR of upgrades takes it to about 26 billion EUR (-25%). An upside of about 80 billion EUR (120 units, full pricing) at a 55 to 60% incremental takes EBIT to about 38 to 39 billion EUR (+11%), and to about 40 billion EUR with GM at 60%.
  • Skew. The cone is wider below consensus than above it for 2028, because the downside combines volume and mix while the upside is capped by supply.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

A901st of 49

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage will add about 13 percentage points of EBIT margin between FY2025 and FY2028 (34.6% to 47.2%), more than the whole of the FY2019 to FY2025 period added (23.6% to 34.6%), but its composition changes: in 2026 it came mostly from gross margin (software upgrades at near-100% margin); in 2027 and 2028 it comes mostly from operating expenses growing at about half the rate of revenue. The leverage is now asymmetric: incremental revenue drops through at about 57%, while a revenue fall would come with a mix shift (fewer upgrades, more under-absorbed capacity) that makes the decremental margin higher, about 65 to 75%. The P&L lines will therefore move up steeply while capacity is sold out and fall more than proportionately in the first year it is not.

The fixed-cost base, sized.

  • Operating expenses: about 6,240 million EUR in FY2026 (Q1 1,488, Q2 1,580, Q3 1,521 on FactSet's gross profit less EBIT; Q4 about 1,650), of which R&D about 4.8 billion and SG&A about 1.5 billion. This is genuinely fixed over a two-year horizon: ASML did not cut R&D in 2009 or in 2023 to 2024.
  • Cost of goods: labour is about 13% of cost of goods (Morgan Stanley, November 2024); about 80% of cost of goods and R&D is outsourced (Moody's); flexworkers are 14% of the labour force; a three-year working-time bank lets the Veldhoven site absorb volume swings without overtime. Materials are variable, but safety stocks and supplier frame contracts make them semi-fixed in cash terms.
  • The new fixed layer is capacity built for 30%-plus annual growth: module-build sites (Wilton, Taiwan), the Brainport campus (first phase 2029, depreciation from then), field-service and installation headcount, and supplier commitments (Zeiss loans of about 1.9 billion EUR and advances of about 1.2 billion EUR, which cannot be cut without stopping EUV output). These costs arrive before the units they serve, which is why management guided Q4 2026 GM (about 57%) below Q3 (57.5%) on higher revenue.

How it shapes the lines (my base case).

  • Gross margin. 51.6% (FactSet basis; 52.8% company basis) in FY2025, 55.7% in FY2026, then a much slower climb: 57.6% (FY2027), 58.3% (FY2028), 58.0% (FY2029), 58.8% (FY2030). Fixed-cost absorption contributes about +0.5 points a year while volume grows 25%, and turns to about -0.5 points in a flat year. The FY2019 to FY2025 band was 43% to 52%; the forecast band is 56% to 59%.
  • Operating expenses. I grow them 14.6% in FY2027 (R&D programmes for EXE, NXE:4200G and Hyper-NA, installation and support hiring, plus about 300 million EUR of retention-award expense) and 8%, 4.5% and 4% thereafter. As a share of revenue they fall from about 17% (FY2025) to 12.8% (FY2027) and 11.1% (FY2028). This is the main source of EBIT margin gain after 2026: of the +5.5 points of EBIT margin from FY2026 to FY2028, about +2.9 points is operating-expense leverage and about +2.6 points is gross margin.
  • EBIT drop-through. Incremental EBIT over incremental revenue: 61% (FY2026), 57% (FY2027), 57% (FY2028), then 29% in FY2029 (revenue +2.8%, operating expenses +4.5%, gross margin -0.3 points) and 63% in FY2030. The 60% management cited for 2027 is achievable but is not exceeded, because the 2026 drop-through was flattered by upgrades.
  • History for contrast. In FY2022 revenue rose 13.8% and EBIT fell 0.5% (supply disruption and cost inflation; GM 49.5%); in FY2024 revenue rose 2.6% and EBIT fell 0.2%. The precedent says flat revenue means flat-to-falling EBIT at ASML even with prices held; it has never been tested by a revenue decline at today's cost base. The 2009 precedent (revenue -46%, GM 31.7% to 24.1%, operating margin -4.9%) shows the extreme but comes from a different business (EUV was not yet in production).

The asymmetry, quantified (my arithmetic on the base case).

  • Upside in FY2028: revenue +7 billion EUR above my base (120 Low-NA units, full Bernstein pricing, IBM 16.5 billion) at a 55 to 60% incremental margin adds about 4 to 4.5 billion EUR of EBIT (+12 to 14%), and supply caps how much more is possible.
  • Downside in FY2028: revenue -10 billion EUR (about 95 Low-NA units, DUV -15%, upgrades falling to about 3.5 billion EUR) loses about 6.5 to 7.5 billion EUR of EBIT (-20 to -23%), because the lost revenue is weighted to upgrades (about 90% margin) and because capacity built for 110-plus units is under-absorbed.
  • A 2029 digestion with revenue -12% instead of my +2.8% would cut FY2029 EBIT by about 30%, to about 23 to 24 billion EUR, because operating expenses sized for 2028 cannot fall in the same year.

Peers. Applied Materials holds a 28 to 33% operating margin through cycles because 23% of revenue is service; Lam's customer-support business (2.5 billion US dollars a quarter) cushioned its memory downturns; KLA holds 37 to 45%. BESI held about 35% through an eleven-quarter downturn on 23 to 26 million EUR of quarterly operating expenses. ASML's model is closest to KLA's (a near-monopoly with software-rich service) but carries a larger, newer manufacturing and supplier footprint, which is why its downturn decremental margin should be read as higher than KLA's, not lower.

What the Consumer should take forward. Leverage explains why EPS can grow about 45% a year to FY2028 on revenue growth of about 29% a year. It also explains why the predictability of the 18-month forecasts rests almost entirely on revenue (units, price, upgrades): a 5% revenue miss in FY2027 costs about 7% of EBIT, and a 5% miss concentrated in IBM costs about 9%.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7688P-0256
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?A881st of 49full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C+5616th of 49

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: there is no financial leverage and there will be none; ASML stays net cash throughout (about 13 billion EUR at end-2026 rising to about 19 to 21 billion EUR by 2028 to 2029 in my base, even after large buybacks). Financial structure shapes EPS in two ways only: net interest and associate income of about 0.5 to 0.75 billion EUR a year (about 1.5 to 2% of pre-tax profit), and buybacks that cut the share count about 2% a year. The balance-sheet feature that behaves like leverage is the customer-funded one: contract liabilities of about 25 billion EUR are a non-interest-bearing liability that funds inventory and buybacks while orders grow and would drain cash, not earnings, if they reversed.

The facts.

  • Q3 2026: cash and short-term investments 12,735 million EUR, debt 1,963 million (down from 4,994 million at end-2024), net cash 10,772 million, net debt to EBITDA -0.66x. Debt maturities are being repaid, not refinanced.
  • Contract liabilities: 18.9 billion EUR at end-2025; about 24.6 billion EUR at Q3 2026 (Bernstein); non-current CL about 9 to 12 billion EUR depending on the source (Bernstein 9.0, CCB International 12.0). Down payments run at 35 to 40% of order value (Bernstein), up from 20 to 30% historically (former ASML manager), which shows the leverage has moved in ASML's favour.
  • Interest: FactSet's net interest expense line is about -130 to -150 million EUR a year, but pre-tax profit has exceeded EBIT (+105 million in FY2025; about +475 million in the first nine months of 2026 excluding Mistral), because interest income and the equity result of the 24.9% Zeiss SMT stake sit below EBIT. Redburn forecasts net interest income of 185 million EUR (2026) and 309 million (2027).

How it shapes EPS (my base).

  • Net financial items: +1,100 million EUR in FY2026 (of which 509.7 million is the Mistral revaluation gain), then +550, +600, +650 and +750 million as net cash builds. Contribution to EPS growth: neutral after 2026 (about +1.5 EUR a share a year), and a 1.3 EUR headwind to FY2027 growth because the Mistral gain does not recur.
  • Buybacks: I size them to free cash flow less dividends, leaving net cash rising slowly: 7.8 billion EUR (FY2026; 6.04 billion already done in nine months), 13 billion (FY2027), 17 billion (FY2028), 13 billion (FY2029, a slower year), 21 billion (FY2030). At assumed average prices of 1,400 to 2,100 EUR this retires about 1.7% of the shares in FY2027 and about 2.3% in FY2028, so the share count adds about 1.5 to 2.0 percentage points a year to EPS growth. Over FY2019 to FY2025 it added about 1.4 points a year.
  • The 12 billion EUR programme for 2026 to 2028 will be exhausted around mid-2027 at the current pace (about 6.1 billion EUR executed in nine months; about 4.1 billion in Q3 alone). I expect a larger programme with the 10 June 2027 Capital Markets Day; consensus's implied share count (about 376 million in FY2028) is too high by about 3%, which is about +2 EUR of FY2028 EPS that consensus is missing.

What could change the balance sheet.

  • An order deferral would reverse the down-payment flow before it reached the P&L: in my base 2029 case, contract liabilities fall about 4 billion EUR and working capital rises about 3 billion, so FCF falls to about 0.71x net income even as EPS rises; buybacks would be slowed first (I cut them to 13 billion EUR in FY2029). The 2023 precedent (FCF 12% of net income, buybacks 0.4 billion EUR in FY2024) shows the pattern.
  • Supplier finance and capacity: further Zeiss capital-expenditure loans or advances (an uncapped commitment above agreed thresholds) and a larger 2028 capacity build would be funded from cash; they reduce buyback capacity, not EPS.
  • The retention award will be settled in shares in 2030 (about 0.6 million shares at today's price), which I add to the share count in FY2030.

Peers. Lam returns 85% of FCF, but funds it from completed deliveries rather than advances; Tokyo Electron funded a 150 billion yen buyback from operating cash; ASM International keeps a minimum cash buffer of 600 million EUR; BESI pays out 40 to 100% of net income and cut its interim dividend 28% in 2026 when earnings fell. ASML is unique in the group in front-running its capital returns with customer money; that is a strength in the up-cycle and the first thing to reverse in a downturn.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7745P-0342
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

D4524th of 49

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: little of the cost base is cuttable quickly without damaging the moat: R&D (about three-quarters of operating expenses) is strategic and was protected in past downturns; SG&A is small; COGS flexes with materials and temporary labour but carries a fixed factory and field base. Within a year ASML could probably remove 0.5 to 0.8 billion EUR of operating expenses (8 to 12%) and some temporary labour and capex, worth one to two points of margin on about 50 billion EUR of revenue. In a downturn the company would protect margin through mix and price discipline more than through cost cuts.

  • R&D (about 4.8 billion EUR a year). EXE, NXE:3800F, 4200G, 1,000-watt source, metrology, e-beam, 3D integration. Programme phasing could defer some spend; cutting it would weaken the generation-by-generation lead. Reducible perhaps 5 to 10% short term.
  • SG&A (about 1.6 billion EUR a year). The delayering is already removing management layers (about 200 million EUR a year of savings from May 2027 per ING). Reducible perhaps 10 to 15%.
  • COGS. Materials (cost of inventories recognised 12.3 billion EUR in FY2025) are variable; ASML presses suppliers for cost-downs. Factory overhead, field-service engineers and D&A are largely fixed in the short run; temporary manufacturing staff are flexible.
  • Capex and supplier investment. Brainport is phased and the 2028 expansion is still being sized, so capex and supplier advances are the most flexible cash lines.
  • Precedent. ASML did not cut R&D in 2023 to 2024; it held price and absorbed a flat year at 51.3% GM (company basis).
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

D4234th of 49

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: very little cost elimination will reach the P&L as a net saving. The January 2026 organisational change is a reallocation from management to engineering, not a cost programme; its roughly 200 million EUR of gross saving arrives from May 2027 and is offset by engineering hiring and by about 300 million EUR a year of retention-award expense (2027 to 2029). The efficiency that will reach the financial statements is in working capital and in cost of goods per unit (cycle time, flow-factory methods, supplier cost-downs), and it reaches cash and gross margin, not operating expenses. I model no net operating-expense saving; operating expenses still fall as a share of revenue because revenue outruns them.

The programme, as it actually stands.

  • Announced January 2026: about 3,000 management and coordination roles out, 1,300 to 1,400 engineering roles in, a net reduction of about 1,700 (about 4% of the workforce). Management said at the April 2026 AGM that the driver was simplification (engineers reporting to two or three managers), not budget.
  • Union agreement (June 2026): fewer cuts than planned; redundancies deferred to May 2027; affected staff have nearly a year to find internal roles; severance capped at 400,000 EUR per person.
  • Saving: ING's estimate of about 200 million EUR a year was made before the union agreement. Morgan Stanley (January 2026) called restructuring costs immaterial and expected R&D growth to flatten only briefly before rising again in 2027. In practice, R&D on the IFRS basis rose from 1.76 billion EUR (H1 2025) to 2.47 billion EUR (H1 2026).
  • My assumption: a gross saving of about 100 million EUR in FY2027 and 150 to 200 million EUR from FY2028, fully absorbed by engineering growth. One-off severance costs of perhaps 50 to 100 million EUR in Q2 2027, too small to adjust out.

Where efficiency will show.

  • Working capital (cash, 2027 to 2030). The cash-conversion cycle is about 300 days against a sub-200-day ambition; gross inventories were 12.2 billion EUR at end-2025. I assume trade working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables) moves from about 41.5% of revenue at end-2026 to 40% by end-2028; each point is about 0.7 billion EUR of cash at FY2028 revenue. I do not assume the ambition is met: cycle-time gains are being consumed by the capacity ramp, and the Brainport flow factory starts only in 2029.
  • Cost of goods per unit (gross margin, 2028 onward). Module-based shipping, AI diagnostics (failure analysis from ten hours to eight minutes), the flow-factory model and supplier cost-downs offset part of the capacity-ramp cost. I credit them only implicitly, inside a gross margin that rises about 0.7 points a year; they are not separately visible.
  • Operating-expense leverage (the real lever). Operating expenses grow 14.6% in FY2027 and 8% in FY2028 against revenue growth of 25.7% and 24.4%: this is leverage, not elimination.

What can be cut in a downturn (relevant for set three's downside cone). Within a year, perhaps 0.5 to 0.8 billion EUR (8 to 12% of operating expenses: temporary staff, SG&A, R&D programme phasing) and 1 to 1.5 billion EUR of capex and supplier advances. That would recover one to two points of EBIT margin on about 60 billion EUR of revenue, not the four to six points a downturn mix shift would remove. ASML's historical response is to hold price and protect R&D, not to cut, so the Consumer should not treat cost flexibility as a cushion for the 2029 downside.

Against the last four to six years. Operating expenses were about 17 to 18% of revenue in FY2023 to FY2025; there was no cost programme in that period either. The difference now is scale: the same R&D intensity choices now produce operating-expense ratios of 11 to 13%.

Peers. AIXTRON cut headcount in early 2026 at a mid-single-digit million EUR one-off cost for an equivalent annual saving; Canon and Nikon run restructurings to rescue weak margins; BESI's lean base (23 to 26 million EUR a quarter) is a design choice. None is a template for ASML, whose problem is not excess cost but the timing of capacity cost.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4790P-0558
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?A901st of 49full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C+5812th of 49

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: four loops reinforce today and two of them will turn balancing inside the forecast window, in a predictable order: first the upgrade-for-cleanroom loop (from late 2027 into 2028, acting on IBM revenue and the gross-margin mix), then the prepayment-to-capital-return loop (2029, acting on free cash flow and buybacks before earnings). The installed-base flywheel keeps reinforcing throughout and is the floor under revenue; the China substitution loop works against ASML slowly until a statutory ban makes it work quickly. The net effect is to front-load the margin and cash gains into 2026 to 2028 and to make 2029 the year in which earnings, cash and the multiple can move in different directions: EPS still up slightly, FCF down about 16%, and the order signal (contract liabilities) negative.

Loop by loop, with the financial line it moves.

  1. Upgrade-for-cleanroom loop (reinforcing now; turns balancing first, late 2027 to 2028). Customers who cannot get cleanroom space or new tools buy productivity software and field upgrades (5 to 7% more throughput, near-100% margin). IBM went from 2,134 million EUR (Q4 2025) to 2,488, 2,762, 3,289 and about 3,700 million (Q1 to Q4 2026): about +49% for the year to about 12.2 billion EUR, and Jefferies attributes nearly 400 basis points of the 2026 GM expansion to it. The loop stops when the cleanrooms under construction come on line (TSMC's N2 and A14 fabs, Samsung P5, SK hynix M15X and Yongin, Micron ID1, mostly 2027 to 2028). My base holds upgrades at about 6.4 billion EUR in 2027 (new products such as PEP packages for 3800E; the CFO is "aggressively developing more upgrades") and lets them fall to about 6.0 billion (2028) and 5.0 billion (2029). Effect: IBM growth slows from +49% to +16%, +6% and +4%; GM loses about 1.3 points of mix by 2028 that pricing has to replace. Early signal: a sequential fall in IBM of more than 20% in Q1 2027 (the first set's threshold) or IBM guided flat for 2027 in January.

  2. Prepayment-to-capital-return loop (reinforcing now; turns balancing second, about 2029). Orders bring 35 to 40% down payments; the cash funds inventory, supplier advances and buybacks (6.1 billion EUR in nine months of 2026; Q3 FCF 11.4 billion EUR with non-current CL up about 5.8 billion). In my base, CL rise about 6.6 billion EUR (2026), 4.5 billion (2027) and 2.0 billion (2028), then fall about 4 billion (2029) as deliveries consume down payments faster than new orders replace them. Effect: FCF/net income 0.91, 0.96, 0.85, then 0.71 in FY2029; buybacks cut from 17 to 13 billion EUR. The cash line warns about a year before the P&L. Early signal: non-current CL falling quarter on quarter (Bernstein's backlog is about 2.2 times CL), or the January 2027 annual backlog below about 45 billion EUR.

  3. Lead-time-to-booking loop (reinforcing, damped by controls). Long lead times invite precautionary orders; ASML's down payments, exit costs and checks against customers' fab plans make cancellation rare and push-outs the failure mode. The 2021 to 2022 shortage followed by 2023 to 2024 digestion is the precedent. Effect in the model: no cancellation risk in 2027; a push-out risk to 2028 to 2029 units, which is why I hold 2028 Low-NA at 110 rather than the 120 to 125 the buy side hoped for and 2029 at only 112.

  4. China substitution loop (balancing against ASML, slow, with a sudden-step risk). Domestic immersion tools (Shanghai Aishengna about 5 units in 2026 and 20 in 2027 at 10 to 20% yields; Yuliangsheng about 12 systems; evaluation at SMIC and Huawei lines) lower the political cost of a US ban; a ban accelerates domestic qualification, as it did in deposition and etch (NAURA and AMEC tools in 5nm-class lines; China's overall WFE self-sufficiency about 21% in 2025). Effect in my base: China falls from about 20% of revenue (2026) to about 15% (2027) and 12 to 13% (2028); China DUV pricing flat. Under the MATCH Act the step is sudden: about -8 to -10% of FY2027 EPS (see the reading notes) and more in a slowdown.

  5. Installed-base flywheel (reinforcing throughout; the floor). Every tool shipped adds service revenue for 20-plus years (about 5% of EUV ASP a year; lifetime service up to 130% of tool value). With 84 to 120 Low-NA systems and 160 to 200 immersion systems a year joining the fleet, service revenue grows about 12 to 15% a year in my base (about 6.8 billion EUR in 2026 to about 11 billion by 2030) even as upgrades fall. Effect: IBM never falls in my base, which caps the 2029 downside: the flywheel alone adds about 1 billion EUR of high-margin revenue a year.

  6. High-NA ecosystem loop (mixed: reinforcing in memory, balancing in logic until about 2030). Intel's volume use (more than one million wafers), Samsung's and SK hynix's DRAM insertion (2028) and the 12-inch mask initiative reinforce; TSMC's Low-NA multi-patterning optimisation delays logic adoption to about 2030. Effect: High-NA revenue stays at about 1.8 billion EUR in 2027, 3.1 billion in 2028, 4.0 billion in 2029 and 5.7 billion in 2030 in my base. The multi-patterning delay is not a loss: it raises Low-NA exposures per wafer, so it shifts revenue from High-NA (low margin today) to Low-NA (high margin), which helps the 2027 to 2028 gross margin.

  7. AI-financing loop (exogenous; the trigger for loops 1 and 2). Hyperscaler FCF near zero in 2026 to 2027 (Jefferies), neocloud credit stress (CoreWeave 2032 bonds below 78 to 80 cents), a third of hyperscaler capex externally financed (Deutsche Bank). Effect: none on 2027 deliveries (prepaid), but it is the variable that decides whether 2028 to 2029 orders firm. Telecom 1998 to 2002 is the precedent for how such loops end: vendor financing and capacity overbuild, then an abrupt capex freeze.

Order of turning, and what it means for the line items. Upgrade loop (IBM growth and GM mix, 2027 to 2028), then the prepayment loop (CL, FCF and buybacks, 2029), then units (2029 if the AI-financing or memory loop turns). Revenue and EPS therefore keep rising through 2028 in my base while their quality (upgrade share, cash conversion) has already begun to fall. The share price, which in this sector de-rates three to six months before the revenue peak, will respond to the first two turns, not the third.

Interplay worth noting. Loops 1 and 6 offset each other in the GM: as upgrades normalise (lower margin mix), High-NA scales (also low margin early), so the 2028 GM has two simultaneous drags that only pricing offsets. Loops 2 and 4 interact through China (a hypothesis, not evidenced in the sources): if Chinese stockpiling orders carried down payments, their normalisation lowers the CL inflow at the same time as it lowers DUV revenue, so the 2029 cash turn could arrive earlier in a MATCH case.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

A862nd of 49

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: current guidance is conservative. The FY2026 initial guide implied about +12% against an out-turn of about +36%; the 2030 framework (44 to 60 billion EUR) sits below FY2028 consensus by about a quarter; and the implicit 2027 frame (fixed units, partial pricing) understates the price-and-mix contribution. Adjusted for input forces that are stronger than in the past (demand exceeding supply, prepaid), the risk of a revenue miss is lower than in 2022 or 2024; the risk that remains is supply execution in 2028, which is the 2022 precedent.

  • Past guidance accuracy: conservative in 2023 and 2025 out-turns; over-optimistic in 2022 (supply) and for FY2025 at the 2024 investor day (demand timing).
  • Present: guidance is set on supply the company controls and on orders largely in hand; the variables that caused past misses (customer push-outs, supply disruption) are mitigated by down payments with exit costs and by the decision not to raise 2027 output until long-lead parts allow.
  • Long range: FY2026 already at the 2030 framework floor; FY2027 consensus near its ceiling. The framework's three scenarios (44, 52, 60 billion EUR) will almost certainly be raised at the 10 June 2027 CMD.
  • Caveat: management has deliberately not guided the 2028 unit number or the price reset's size; conservative guidance on known items coexists with unquantified items that could disappoint the highest private expectations (buy-side 120 to 125 Low-NA units in 2028).

Judgement: four features are uncommon and material: the withdrawal of quarterly bookings disclosure at the peak of order momentum, the use of a scenario-based long-range framework that is left unchanged through cycles, the reliance on customer down payments as the visible demand signal, and the dual-GAAP reporting with a non-operating revaluation in 2026 EPS.
