11d

Current multiple

B827th of 49Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0744
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4426th of 49

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the share price is about three-fifths industry-driven and two-fifths company-driven on a rolling six-month basis (correlation with the equal-weighted dashboard peer basket 0.79, R-squared 62%, idiosyncratic share 38%), and the beta to that basket has fallen from 1.45 two years ago to 0.76 now, so ASML has become the low-beta member of the European semiconductor group rather than its amplifier. The multiple, by contrast, is more company-driven than the price: ASML's twenty-four-month-forward P/E (19.7x on the financials block's roll, 2nd percentile of five years; 21.8x on the dashboard's roll, 32nd percentile of ten years) has compressed further than its peers' on their own histories because ASML's earnings have risen faster than any of theirs, not because its price fell more. The practical reading: a sector drawdown of 20% would take ASML down about 15% whatever January says; the 38% that is ASML's own is what the January backlog and the June 2027 unit number can move; and the de-rating is a sector phenomenon applied to a company whose estimate momentum is the sector's strongest.

The co-movement data, read. Latest window (4 May to 26 October 2026): correlation 0.79, beta 0.76, R-squared 62%; six-month return +17.3% against the basket's +2.1%. Over 24 month-ends the correlation ranged 0.70 to 0.79 (lowest in October to December 2025, when the AI re-rating ran without earnings momentum; highest now and in May to June 2025 and January 2026), the R-squared 48% to 63%, and the beta fell almost monotonically from 1.45 (November 2024) to 0.76. Pairwise, ASML moves most with ASM International (0.86; R-squared 74%), VAT Group (0.76), BESI (0.75), AIXTRON and COMET (0.70), Infineon (0.69) and least with Technoprobe and X-FAB (0.42), Pfeiffer (0.23) and Soitec and AMS Osram (0.48). So "the sector" that drives ASML is the front-end equipment subset (ASM, VAT, BESI), not the chip makers; the relevant peer narrative is the equipment cycle's, which is the de-rate-before-peak playbook.

What the falling beta means. A beta of 0.76 to a basket that includes ASM International (0.74 pairwise beta) and VAT (0.85) says ASML's daily moves have become smaller than its closest peers' in both directions: it has been treated as the safe way to own the theme. That is consistent with the crowding evidence (76% Buy, consensus top pick at Morgan Stanley and Bernstein, the heaviest daily traded value in the group at about 2 billion US dollars) and with the technical reading (momentum D after the October break, while relative strength sits at the 82nd percentile of the universe and the 200-day average has risen in each of the last twelve months). A low beta on the way down is comforting; a low beta on the way up is the cost of being the consensus holding. Over six months ASML's +17.3% against the basket's +2.1% shows the idiosyncratic 38% has lately worked in its favour (the July and October beat-and-raise prints).

The multiple against peers' multiples. On the dashboard's common basis (current P/E and its percentile against each stock's own ten-year history): ASML 21.8x at the 32nd percentile; ASM International 19.5x at the 45th; BESI 17.6x at the 36th; VAT 34.6x at the 68th; AIXTRON 18.2x at the 25th; Infineon 12.8x at the 16th; COMET 17.3x at the 15th. ASML's percentile is below ASM's and VAT's although its twelve-month price return (+55.8%) exceeds theirs (+25.9%, +68.2% for VAT), which can only be because its forward earnings rose more (FY2027 EPS consensus +79% in twelve months; FY2028 +106%). On the five-year series the financials block puts ASML at the 2nd percentile (19.7x against a range of 18.1x to 40.4x, median 24.9x); the Selector's ten-year bands put 19.0x as the "crash" band and 23.6x as the median. Deutsche Bank applies a 26x 2028 target multiple (a 20% premium to peers) and ASMI 27x; Morgan Stanley argues 22 to 25x two-year-forward is the instructive mid-cycle range; Jefferies expects a de-rating to the low 20s on its 2027 basis. So the multiple is at the bottom of its own range while peers sit nearer their middles: the compression is company-specific in origin (earnings outran price) and sector-specific in cause (the market refuses to capitalise the 2028 peak), which is the configuration the first set's C-84 called "a debate to buy into".

How far the multiple is company-driven, in practice. Three tests. (i) Direction over six months: ASML's price rose 17% while its forward P/E fell (estimates up more); the basket's price rose 2% with mixed estimates. Company-driven. (ii) Daily variance: 62% shared. Industry-driven. (iii) Events: the two largest single-day moves in the period were ASML's own (the July results; the 27 July 2026 China DUV announcement that cut the shares about 7% and, per the own-5 report, "wiped out over 1 trillion US dollars" across semiconductors, so even the idiosyncratic shock propagated to the sector). Mixed. My weighting: the level of the multiple is about half company-driven (the 2028-peak debate is about ASML's own capacity and pricing numbers) and half sector-driven (the AI-financing and de-rating regime); the day-to-day path is three-fifths sector-driven.

What this implies for predictability and for the memo's phase judgement (topics #10 and #11). The earnings are predictable (D-04) and the multiple is not: on the sector's precedents (multiples compress 40 to 50% after their cycle peak, with compression averaging 79 weeks and consensus cuts lagging the price peak by about 37 weeks per Deutsche Bank's 2022 study), the multiple has already done most of a cycle's compression (about 40x to 20x) while the estimates have not yet peaked. That is the "turning up inside a de-rating" configuration: fundamentals in an upgrade phase, price in a digestion phase. A sector regime change (credit spreads narrowing, Semiconductors moving off Underweight) would lift ASML by its 62% shared component regardless of its own news; a January backlog above about 50 billion EUR and a June unit number above 110 would lift the 38%. The memo's fair-multiple view should therefore be anchored on ASML's own range (the median 23.6x on the ten-year bands) rather than on peers' current multiples, because peers' multiples are not compressed and ASML's is.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

A644th of 49

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the precedents say semiconductor-equipment shares make most of their money from multiple expansion early in an up-cycle and lose it through multiple compression three to six months before the revenue peak, regardless of earnings; ASML has already de-rated in the expected way while its earnings visibility has lengthened, which is the configuration the sector's own rule of thumb treats as "a debate to buy into". ASML fits the de-rating pattern on price and fits the outperformance pattern on fundamentals; the open question is whether the feared peak year (2028) keeps being planned up.

The patterns, with the evidence:

  • Multiple leads earnings. More than 80% of European semicap share-price gains since late September 2025 came from re-rating rather than earnings upgrades (Jefferies). Semiconductor multiples typically contract by 50% or more three to six months before the revenue-cycle peak, which Jefferies models for Q4 2026. Equipment shares "de-rate before peak earnings" (sector digest).
  • Precedents in the cohort. BESI de-rated 46% (34x to 19x EV/EBIT) between May and September 2026; VAT from 37x to 26x; ASML's own multiple fell from about 40x (five-year high) to about 18 to 20x on twenty-four-month-forward earnings while consensus FY2027 EPS rose 79% in twelve months.
  • ASML-specific precedents. Growth years in pairs and triples followed by digestion (FY2021 +33% then +14%; FY2023 +30% then +2.6%); guidance resets that validated near-term bears (July 2022 growth cut to 10% from 20% on supply; October 2024 FY2025 range cut to 30 to 35 billion EUR with a 300 basis point GM cut and a share fall of more than 16% in a day); multiples peak and de-rate as soon as quarterly order books decline (Jefferies). Over the long run, every claim that lithography intensity would permanently decline has been wrong.
  • What generated outperformance. (i) Earnings-upgrade cycles early in an up-cycle (2026 is one: FY2026 EPS consensus +59% in twelve months); (ii) re-rating when visibility extends beyond the market's feared peak (the sector digest's rule: "watch whether the feared peak year is being planned up; 2028 is, which makes the pause a debate to buy into"); (iii) a monopoly premium that returns when growth visibility extends (Bernstein notes the premium to US semicap disappeared for the first time).
  • What generated underperformance. Peak-cycle capacity additions (the semicap archetype), pull-forward and double-ordering (the 2021 to 2022 shortage precedent preceding the 2024 correction), and export-control shocks.

Fit. On price, ASML is in the de-rating phase (technical momentum D; 2nd percentile multiple). On fundamentals, it is in the upgrade phase with lengthening visibility. The base rate argues for caution on 2029, not on 2026 to 2028. For the model, the precedents argue for an explicit digestion scenario in 2029 (flat to modestly down revenue) carried alongside the structural case, rather than a straight-line extrapolation.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C-5021st of 49

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: on unchanged consensus earnings, the multiple is already close to its trough, so the multiple-only downside is about 8 to 17% depending on the forward-earnings roll; the larger downside is earnings-led. A fair trough multiple (about 18 to 20 times twenty-four-month-forward earnings) on a plateau or China-ban earnings base gives about 850 to 1,100 EUR (-22% to -40%); a full cycle downturn gives about 750 EUR (-47%).

  • Where the multiple sits. 19.7x twenty-four-month-forward consensus on the financials block (2nd percentile of five years; five-year low 18.1x) or 21.8x on the dashboard's roll (32nd percentile of ten years). The Selector's ten-year "crash" band is 19.0x (10th percentile).
  • Multiple-only (earnings unchanged). To 18.1x on the block's roll: about -8%. To 18x on the dashboard's 64.69 EUR: about 1,165 EUR, -17%. To a semicap trough near 15x (the level Jefferies cites as the historical trough for ASM International): about 970 to 1,070 EUR, -24% to -31%.
  • Earnings plus multiple. Plateau (FY2028 EPS about 60 EUR, roughly the prior memo's path) at 18x: about 1,080 EUR (-23%). MATCH case (Jefferies: FY2027 EPS about 44 EUR) at 20 to 24x: about 880 to 1,050 EUR (-25% to -37%). Morgan Stanley's full-cycle bear (FY2028 EPS 37.5 EUR at a 20x cycle-bottom): 750 EUR (-47%). Options in July priced about a 4% probability of the shares below 750 EUR within a year.
  • Precedent. Within the last twelve months the shares more than doubled from a 52-week low of 818 EUR to a high of 1,739 EUR and have since fallen 19%; BESI fell 46% on multiple alone in 2026; Jefferies' rule of thumb is a de-rating of about 50% before the cycle's revenue peak.
  • Reading for the case. Because the multiple is already near trough on consensus, further downside requires consensus earnings to fall; the share price is therefore now more exposed to 2028 estimate risk than to multiple risk.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

A603rd of 49

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative staircase is good and dated, with the June 2027 CMD as the step most able to re-set the market's frame from "cycle peak in 2028" to "growth through 2030". Near-term steps (January guide, backlog, buyback, pricing) are supportive but partly expected; the negative-side dates (November truce expiries, the year-end defence bill, memory price data) cluster in the next three months, which is consistent with the current risk-off reading of the sector. The staircase strengthens the case for a base model that runs above the old 2030 framework, provided the January backlog and 2028 unit plan confirm it.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–80

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two sets are highly congruent on the structure of the case and on the base case through FY2028; the few places where they jar are not contradictions of fact but of emphasis, and in each the second set's later, more arithmetical reading should prevail. The one incongruence that would have misled the memo is inside the model rather than the answers (the free-cash-flow gap), and it is corrected. Congruence is itself evidence for predictability: when a monopoly's supply constraint, its pricing, its installed-base annuity and its customers' prepayments all point to the same 2027, the forecast rests on several independent legs.

Where the sets align, and why that matters. Six judgements recur in both sets and in every AlphaSense report, each supported by a different kind of evidence: (i) 2027 revenue is a price, mix and installed-base question, not a units question, because Low-NA capacity is fixed at about 85 by long-lead parts and close to fully ordered (company statements; Deutsche Bank 15 October 2026; Bernstein's 56 billion EUR backlog inference with 2027 EUV "fully covered"); (ii) 2028 is where the debate lives, because consensus already embeds about 110 units and the buy side wanted 120 to 125 (Morgan Stanley 15 October 2026); (iii) the margin path is a tug between near-100%-margin upgrades (about 400 basis points of 2026 expansion, Jefferies) that normalise, value-based pricing that lands partially in 2027 and fully in 2028, and High-NA and capacity-ramp dilution; (iv) policy is the one force that can move revenue by decree, bounded at about a fifth of revenue with servicing the hidden part; (v) the company's own base rate says growth runs end in digestion, so 2029 carries a genuine probability of a pause or decline; (vi) the multiple has de-rated before the feared peak while estimates rose, the sector's standard pattern. The first set derived these from the foundations; the second set reproduced them from the arithmetic of units, prices and margins; the AlphaSense reports written for this set (T-D1, T-D2) find the same three variables as the dominant drivers. Three routes to one answer is the strongest form of congruence available.

Where they jar, and how I resolve each.

  1. The FY2027 cushion (resolved in favour of the second set). The first set called FY2027 consensus "undemanding on run-rate grounds"; the second set showed the annualisation error (Q4 is about 30 to 33% of the year) and re-rated FY2027 as "achievable but not undemanding". I agree with the second set; the T-D2 report independently documents the Q4-to-Q1 decline (about -11% into Q1 2026) and the December 2025 China pull-forward that inflated Q4 2025. The memo should not inherit the first set's phrase.
  2. The size and timing of the MATCH Act's effect (resolved by splitting magnitude from timing). The first set carried Jefferies' framing (about 15% of revenue; MATCH-case FY2027 EPS 44 EUR against 64); the second set argued re-allocation while immersion is short cuts the hit to about 8 to 10% of EPS. The own-2 report supports re-allocation in principle (the 2023 licence revocation was backfilled; change orders convert blocked tools to compliant models) but shows it takes seven to twelve months and depends on receiving cleanrooms. Morgan Stanley's view that implementation takes a year or more after enactment pushes the effect into 2028. My resolution: magnitude as the second set (8 to 10% of a full year's EPS, larger in a slowdown), timing later than the second set assumed, so the 18-month window carries the risk mostly in its last two quarters.
  3. Predictability of fundamentals against predictability of the share price (not a contradiction, but the memo must keep them apart). The first set's risk group scored 40 and its perception question 30; the second set's forecast group scored 70. Both are right: the earnings path is unusually trackable and the share price is unusually sector-driven (D-07: 62% of daily variance explained by the peer basket). The first set's C-83 "multiple-only downside 8 to 17%" and C-82 "perception can move the shares 10 to 20%" are the same statement from two sides.
  4. Disclosure narrowed, yet guidance is specific (both true). The first set's C-14 and C-60 record the withdrawal of quarterly bookings; its C-72 scores guidance specificity at 85. The second set then uses contract liabilities as the bookings proxy, which the own-3 report validates (Bernstein's regression: backlog about 2.2 times total CL, r-squared 0.96) while warning that fast shipments and accounting changes have distorted CL before (2022 to 2023). The resolution is a monitoring rule: read non-current CL, not total CL, as the order signal.
  5. The retention award (corrected by the second set, confirmed here). The first set treated the delayering as a cost lever; the second set added the roughly 300 million EUR a year of share-award expense for 2027 to 2029 that cancels it. The own-6 report confirms straight-line expensing over three years and settlement from treasury shares. Congruent after correction.
  6. High-NA: "doubt disproved" (first set) against "only 1.85 billion EUR in 2027" (second set). Not a jar: production readiness at Intel and a DRAM insertion date at Samsung (2028) establish the platform; TSMC's 2030 adoption and the 12-inch mask timetable (pilot 2031) keep the revenue small and the margin dilutive within the horizon. Both sets say this; the T-D1 report's NXE:3300 precedent (zero gross margin on the first eleven systems; corporate GM trough of 41.8% in 2017) supports the second set's margin path.
  7. Share count (congruent, but the model disagreed with both). The first set wanted 1.5 to 2.5% a year of decline; the second set sized buybacks to FCF (about 2.2% a year by 2028). The model, lacking the down-payment line, shows those buybacks draining net cash to 8.8 billion EUR by FY2028, which would make the second set's share count look reckless. With the corrected working-capital input the model and both sets agree.

What the congruence does not cover. Neither set tested the two assumptions the T-D2 report singles out as unprecedented: how far upgrade revenue falls when cleanroom tightness ends (no house quantifies 10%, 30% or 50%), and whether a 30%-plus unit ramp can coincide with gross-margin expansion (ASML's 2017 and 2022 ramps both saw GM fall). Those are the two gaps the fourth set and the research requests should carry.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C+5514th of 49

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the sell-side narrative articulates most of the dynamics our assessment rests on, and articulates the three that decide 2028 (the unit number against 120 to 125, the size of the price reset, the cyclicality of upgrades) prominently and from several houses; it articulates the AI-financing loop prominently at the macro and credit desks and only loosely at the equipment desks; and it articulates five of our dynamics thinly or not at all: the order in which the reinforcing loops turn (upgrades, then down payments, then units), the cash-before-earnings shape of 2029, the retention-award expense, the servicing share of China exposure by designated entity, and the base-effect deceleration of reported growth through 2027 as a trap. Investor commentary (Twitter, buy-side letters) is not covered by any source available to this set, so I cannot credit or discredit it; the AlphaSense report written for this question did not reach it, and I record that as a gap and a research request. For the next 18 months the known dynamics dominate (the window is well mapped); for 18 months to three years the unknown ones dominate (no house models a 2029 decline, and only Jefferies frames the de-rating playbook explicitly for ASML).

Coverage check. The T-I2 report names, with their own documents, Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Deutsche Bank, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Huatai, CCB International, ING, Eugene, Kiwoom and Hana; the financials block shows about thirty contributing brokers, of which the named set is about a third and includes the houses that set the debate. The report did not state how many houses AlphaSense holds research for or the share covered, and it holds no investor-commentary source; I treat its sell-side findings as representative of the European and Asian coverage and silent on the buy side.

Dynamic by dynamic: who says it, how prominently, since when, with what view, and how well known.

  1. Upgrade cyclicality inside IBM, and the effect of new cleanrooms. Widely articulated. Jefferies is the standard-bearer (16 July 2026: normalised supply would cut upgrade revenue; 14 October: about 400 basis points of 2026 GM from software, 2028 GM flat at 58.2% or 55.2% if upgrades fall); Morgan Stanley (15 October) and Bernstein (14 October) take the resilient side (upgrades bypass hardware lead times; a structural opportunity); CCB and Huatai note the margin lift; Deutsche Bank names ramp costs as the other offset. The 2023 precedent (IBM guidance cut to flat; the Q4 2023 to Q1 2024 drop) and the 2018 utilisation point (Wennink: customers will not take tools offline at peak) are in the record. Well known for 18 months. Not known: the size of the fall when cleanrooms open; the T-D2 and own-4 reports both record that no house quantifies it and no precedent at this scale exists.
  2. Buybacks' reliance on down payments; what a fall in contract liabilities signals. Moderately articulated and recent. Bernstein (19 October 2026) built the CL-to-backlog regression and the 35 to 40% down-payment figure; CCB (15 October) tied the 4.1 billion EUR Q3 buyback to 11.4 billion EUR of FCF from down payments; Deutsche Bank's 2023 note ("Pushout window") and its January 2025 note record the April 2023 to late 2024 buyback pause and the 7.8 billion EUR Q4 2024 cash surge from prepayments; Jefferies (15 October) warns deferred orders would reduce down payments. The 2029 shape in our base (FCF down about 16% while EPS still rises; buybacks cut from 17 to 13 billion EUR) is not stated anywhere; the Morgan Stanley counter (12.7 billion EUR of cash insulates buybacks) is the common view.
  3. MATCH Act: re-allocation while immersion is short, and the servicing share. Widely articulated since April 2026. Jefferies (28 July: a 15% revenue headwind; a low-teens share of system sales), Bernstein (6 April and 19 October: re-allocation absorbs diverted tools; 20% China exposure limits the downside), Morgan Stanley (8 and 28 September: the defence bill as the vehicle; a year or more to implement; September 2024: up to 10 to 15% of IBM at risk, 35% of the immersion fleet in China), Redburn (9 April: servicing is the material part), Deutsche Bank (July 2024: servicing pressure), Hana (16 October: standalone passage limited). Well known in direction and headline size; the re-allocation lead time (seven to twelve months) and the designated-entity split of China revenue and servicing are stated by no house with figures (own-2).
  4. Base-effect deceleration of reported growth through 2027; the Q1 decline. Thinly articulated. Bernstein (29 January 2026) documented the about -11% Q1 2026 guide; Eugene (15 October) models 2027 growth of 28% against 34%; CCB notes Q1 seasonality; Jefferies (29 September) frames the semiconductor revenue cycle peaking in Q4 2026 on memory-price base effects and the three-to-six-month de-rating lead. No house frames ASML's own Q3 and Q4 2027 prints (+20%, +8% in our base) as a false peak signal, and none quantifies the usual Q4-to-Q1 decline as a range (-10% to -16%). Partly known as a sector pattern; not known as an ASML calendar.
  5. Size of the price reset and the flow-through after ramp costs. Universally articulated qualitatively since 14 October 2026 (every house); sized only by Bernstein (EUV ASP +10% in 2027, +7% in 2028; 2028 GM 58.9%) and bounded by Deutsche Bank (60% by Q4 2028 after ramp costs) and Redburn (some absorbed by input costs and launch expenses); Jefferies carries a flat 2028. Well known in direction; the percentage is unknown to all and will remain so until the CMD, if then.
  6. The retention award's expense. Not articulated by any sell-side source in the record; the only sources are the annual report's accounting policy and the own-6 report's derivation (about 300 million EUR a year, straight-line, 2027 to 2029, settled from treasury shares). Unknown. It is small (under 3% of net income) but it cancels the delayering saving that several houses credit.
  7. 2028 Low-NA output against the 120 to 125 hoped for. The central debate, articulated by every house since 15 October 2026: Morgan Stanley (the gap to buy-side hopes; "capacity remains the key debate"), Deutsche Bank (85 fixed; above 110 constrained by module-build), Jefferies (109 units; power constraints), Bernstein (15 September: "110 is not a supply cap"), Redburn (de-bottlenecking drives 35 to 40% system-sales growth), Huatai (2028 EUV capacity to be raised). Fully known; the number itself is unknown.
  8. High-NA timing. Fully known (Intel in volume; Samsung DRAM 2028; SK hynix 2028; TSMC 2030; the 12-inch mask timetable). The margin dilution (about 20% early) is known from Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank.
  9. The probability and timing of a 2029 digestion year. Articulated as a pattern by Jefferies (the de-rating playbook; "2028 trends are less certain"), Morgan Stanley (multiples de-rate into the low 20s as tightness eases; the 2017 Asian precedent), Deutsche Bank's 2022 cycle work (79 weeks of compression); countered by Kiwoom (orders requested to 2030), Bernstein (the 1c DRAM super-cycle extends the up-cycle) and Maybank (a "profit plateau"). Known as a risk; not modelled: consensus FY2029 is +13.5% revenue and +20% EPS, and no visible broker carries a 2029 decline. The 18-to-36-month narrative is therefore where our assessment differs most from the record.
  10. The AI-financing loop from neocloud credit to equipment orders. Prominently articulated at the macro, credit and digital-infrastructure desks (Morgan Stanley 25 and 27 October: the quality divide; data-centre financing; CoreWeave's reliance on external capital; Deutsche Bank 27 October: "the forecast is neocloudy"; Redburn 21 September: credit markets price what equities ignore; Jefferies 27 October: debt markets shut for neoclouds without non-AI revenue) and acknowledged at the equipment desks as the reason for the October de-rating (Deutsche Bank: peers down 29% from July highs by mid-October; Morgan Stanley: a sharp correction on financing fears). The transmission to ASML's 2028 to 2029 order book is not modelled by any equipment analyst; the T-I2 report calls it an analytical blind spot in equity models. Known as a sector sentiment driver; unknown as a fundamental input.
  11. Peers. Applied, Lam, KLA and Tokyo Electron: upgrade cycles and China exposure are standard coverage (Lam's 35% China mix; KLA's 80%-plus service attach; Tokyo Electron's field-solutions sensitivity to utilisation); the cycle-peak debate is framed for all four by Jefferies and Deutsche Bank. ASM International and BESI: the peak-cycle and adoption-date debates are the reason both de-rated (BESI -54% from June; Deutsche Bank 27 October) and are fully articulated. VAT Group: record orders and nine-month lead times are read as both confirmation of the ramp and an inventory risk (Jefferies). Nikon and Canon: the second-source immersion platform (2028 prototype, 10 to 20% share ambition) and the packaging niche are covered by Jefferies and Morgan Stanley; nanoimprint is noted and discounted.

What this means for the memo's topics #7 and #8. The narrative is mature on everything that resolves inside the window; the scope for the narrative to move in our favour over six to twelve months lies in three places where it is thin: the official backlog in January (converting Bernstein's inference into fact), the CMD's 2028 and 2030 numbers (converting the capacity debate into a plan), and the upgrade-mix evidence through 2027 (if IBM holds, the Jefferies flat-margin case loses its anchor). The scope for it to move against us lies where the narrative is also thin: the 2029 decline probability and the cash-before-earnings turn, which no house carries and which the first down-quarter in non-current CL would make visible at once. Momentum in narrative terms is therefore positive on substance and fragile on sentiment: a crowded Buy share, a technical break, a sector Underweight and an AI-financing debate that the equity desks acknowledge but do not model.

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 11:49 to 12:08 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
ASML (ASML-NL): answers to the first set of questions (foundational components)

Written 2026-10-25 for the Analyst building the 18-month to four-year operating and financial model.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (conventions, anchor numbers and data traps)

Abbreviations used throughout. EUV: extreme-ultraviolet lithography (Low-NA = NXE platform; High-NA = EXE platform). DUV: deep-ultraviolet lithography (immersion = ArF immersion, NXT platform). IBM: Installed Base Management (service, field upgrades and software sold into the installed fleet). WFE: wafer-fab equipment spending. GM: gross margin. CL: contract liabilities (customer down payments). CMD: Capital Markets Day. All money in EUR unless stated.

Anchor numbers (as at 2026-10-23 to 2026-10-25).

  • Price 1,407.2 EUR; 52-week range 818 to 1,739; 200-day average 1,381 (price +1.9% above it); 50-day 1,507; market capitalisation about 539 billion EUR on 382.8 million diluted shares; enterprise value about 524 billion EUR; net cash 10,772 million EUR at Q3 2026.
  • FY2026 as guided after Q3: revenue 44 to 45 billion EUR (nine months 29,755 million; Q4 guide 14.2 to 15.2 billion, midpoint 14.7 billion); GM about 56% (Q3 57.5%, Q4 guide about 57%); Q4 IBM about 3.7 billion EUR.
  • Reported basic EPS for nine months 27.69 EUR (7.15, 7.59, 12.95). Q3 includes a 509.7 million EUR Mistral AI revaluation gain (about 1.3 EUR a share); Q3 EPS excluding it was about 11.6 EUR. My arithmetic for Q4 at the guide midpoint (14.7 billion revenue, 57% GM, about 1.65 billion operating expenses, about 17.5% tax) gives about 14.6 EUR, so FY2026 lands near 42 EUR reported and near 41 EUR excluding Mistral.
  • FactSet consensus medians: revenue 44,464 / 57,507 / 73,683 / 83,599 million EUR (FY2026 to FY2029); EBIT 18,577 / 25,847 / 34,646 / 40,418 (margins 41.8%, 44.9%, 47.0%, 48.3%); EPS 42.49 / 57.55 / 76.44 / 91.72 (growth +71.8%, +35.5%, +32.8%, +20.0%); gross profit 24,760 / 33,268 / 42,408 (55.7%, 57.9%, 57.6%); FCF 14,642 / 18,266 / 25,249; capex 1,862 / 2,209 / 2,400; dividend 8.62 / 10.21 / 13.13 EUR.
  • Segment consensus (one, two and three years forward): EUV systems 17,335 / 23,844 / 32,343; DUV systems 14,584 / 18,231 / 22,121; High-NA 1,400 / 1,400 / not given; IBM 11,724 / 13,901 / 15,384 million EUR.

Data traps the Consumer must not fall into.

  • FY2029 consensus rests on four to eight brokers. The FY2029 gross-profit figure (42,408 million, identical to FY2028) and the FY2029 EBITDA margin of 54% are artefacts of thin coverage; do not use them.
  • The FY2026 IBM consensus (11,724 million) is stale: the company's own trajectory (Q1 2,488; Q2 2,762; Q3 3,289; Q4 guide about 3,700) sums to about 12.2 billion EUR, roughly +49% on FY2025's 8,193 million.
  • Two forward price-to-earnings series disagree because of the forward roll: the financials block reads 19.7x on twenty-four-month-forward consensus (2nd percentile of five years; five-year range 18.1x to 40.4x, median 24.9x); the dashboard reads 21.8x on 64.69 EUR of twenty-four-month-forward EPS (32nd percentile of ten years). The Selector's ten-year bands are 19.0x ("crash", 10th percentile), 22.1x (35th), 23.6x (median), 26.5x (75th), 29.4x (90th).
  • Gross margin FY2025 is 51.6% on the FactSet basis and 52.8% on the company basis; use one basis consistently. EPS appears on four bases; consensus is on the brokers' basis (FY2025 base 24.73 EUR).
  • The Assistant Portfolio Manager's note of 23 October says the price "came to the memo's 1,200 euro level"; the dashboard data block (the designated price source) shows 1,407.2 EUR on 23 October. I use the dashboard figure.
  • The prior memo (2026-09-23) modelled EPS of 39.0 / 51.5 / 62 / 68 EUR for FY2026 to FY2029. Its FY2026 figure has already been overtaken (about 41 to 42 EUR now looks right) and its plateau assumptions should be re-tested, not inherited.

The four judgements that matter most for the model, in one place.

  1. 2027 revenue is largely a price, mix and installed-base question, not a units question: Low-NA EUV capacity is fixed at about 85 systems by long-lead components. FY2027 consensus (57.5 billion EUR) is only about 9% above the H2 2026 annualised run-rate (11.66 + 14.7 = 26.4 billion EUR, so about 52.7 billion EUR annualised) and below the Q4 2026 guided run-rate (14.7 billion EUR a quarter, about 58.8 billion EUR annualised), while Low-NA units rise about 30%. FY2027 consensus is therefore undemanding unless IBM upgrades or China DUV fall back sharply.
  2. 2028 is where the debate lives: consensus FY2028 already embeds about 110 Low-NA systems at mid-single-digit ASP growth (EUV systems 32.3 billion EUR is consistent with about 110 units at about 270 million EUR plus a High-NA contribution). Upside comes from units above 110 (management said a 30% increase "will not meet demand"; Morgan Stanley infers more than 110 but below the 120 to 125 the buy side wants) and the full effect of value-based pricing (completed negotiations; partial in 2027, full from 2028). Downside comes from upgrade normalisation, High-NA dilution (about 20% GM on early systems per Morgan Stanley) and any memory or AI-financing pause.
  3. The gross-margin path is a three-way tug: software upgrades at about 100% GM added roughly 400 basis points to 2026 GM expansion (Jefferies) and are cyclical; value-based pricing adds from 2027 and fully from 2028; High-NA mix and capacity-ramp costs subtract. Consensus has GM flat at about 57.6% to 57.9% for FY2027 and FY2028, which assumes these net to zero.
  4. The equity is priced as if the cycle peaks: twenty-four-month-forward P/E at the 2nd percentile of five years while FY2027 EPS consensus has risen 37% in six months and 79% in twelve. The gap between sell-side momentum (A) and technical momentum (D) is the market's financing-and-peak fear, not a demand signal.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: ASML Holding N.V. (ASML-NL; ASML), the Dutch sole supplier of EUV lithography systems and leading supplier of DUV immersion and dry lithography, metrology and inspection tools; reports two lines, Net System Sales (EUV Low-NA and High-NA, DUV, metrology) and Installed Base Management (service, field upgrades and productivity software for the installed fleet).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (the WFE oligopoly, whose margins and cost actions through the 2019 and 2023 downturns give the closest dated precedents); ASM International (Dutch front-end equipment maker with high R&D intensity and a capacity build in Arizona and Korea); BE Semiconductor (capital-light assembly model with documented margin resilience through a 14-quarter downturn); AIXTRON (highly geared equipment maker with a two-reduction cost programme in 2025 to 2026); Canon and Nikon (lithography competitors, Nikon's precision segment loss-making); Carl Zeiss SMT (private sole supplier of ASML's optics, whose capacity costs feed into ASML's cost of goods). Inputs: What share of ASML's cost of goods sold is fixed (factory overhead, field-service engineers, depreciation, warranty) versus variable (materials, temporary labour), and how has that split changed as Low-NA EUV capacity is raised from about 65 systems in 2026 toward about 85 in 2027 and more than 110 in 2028 (company statements, 15 July and 14 October 2026)? How did ASML's gross and operating margins move with revenue in 2009, 2012 to 2013, 2019 and 2023 to 2024, and what were the decremental margins? How much of the 2026 gross-margin increase (guided 51 to 53 percent in January 2026, about 56 percent in October 2026) came from fixed-cost absorption, product mix, and installed-base software upgrades; Jefferies (14 October 2026) attributes nearly 400 basis points to productivity-upgrade software; what do other brokers and experts say? What capacity-ramp, supplier-investment and NXE:3800F launch costs did management cite for guiding Q4 2026 gross margin to about 57 percent after 57.5 percent in Q3, and how large and how persistent are they? What is the current and expected gross margin on High-NA EXE systems (Morgan Stanley estimates about 20 percent) and on what volume path does it reach corporate levels? What did the January 2026 organisational transformation (announced as a net reduction of about 1,700 positions; ING estimated about 200 million EUR of annual savings) actually deliver after the June 2026 union agreement deferred redundancies to May 2027? Which cost lines (R&D, SG&A, field service, temporary staff, capex, supplier advances and interest-free loans to Zeiss) did ASML and its peers prove able to cut in past downturns, which did they protect, and how long did it take? How did operating expenses as a share of revenue behave at peers in prior up-cycles when revenue grew more than 25 percent a year for two to three years? Focus: How much of ASML's fixed cost base is now sized for 30 percent-plus annual capacity growth, and therefore what decremental margin a 2028 to 2029 digestion would produce, set against how much further operating leverage remains if revenue keeps rising through 2028.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (pricing and upgrade-pricing behaviour in WFE through cycles); Canon and Nikon (direct lithography competitors whose DUV pricing, helped by yen weakness, sets the mature-node price floor); Shanghai Aishengna and SMEE (state-backed Chinese immersion DUV entrants, pricing inside China); ASM International and BE Semiconductor (European equipment makers using value-based pricing and price increases to pass on cost inflation); Carl Zeiss SMT and TRUMPF (private sole suppliers whose cost increases ASML must absorb or pass on). Operating conditions: A supply-constrained up-cycle with customers pre-paying (2021 to 2022, and 2026 onward); a cyclical digestion with flat or falling tool demand (2019, 2023 to 2024); a memory-led demand peak followed by a memory price break; new-product transitions where throughput rises (NXE:3400 to 3600 to 3800E to 3800F; High-NA EXE:5000 to 5200); export-control tightening that restricts sales and servicing in China; supplier and input-cost inflation (2022, and rising raw-material and labour costs in 2026); a strong euro against the dollar and yen while ASML bills in euros. Inputs: What is the size, product coverage, timing and backlog coverage of the value-based pricing negotiations the CFO described as completed on 14 October 2026 (partial benefit in 2027, full from 2028; covering critical high-volume products, associated upgrades and services; not to be repeated annually), and what do customers, experts and brokers estimate the increase to be? How have EUV average selling prices moved per wafer-per-hour of throughput gained since the NXE:3400, and what price premium does the NXE:3800F (260 wafers an hour against 230 for the 3800E) carry; Bernstein (30 July 2026) projects EUV ASPs up about 10 percent in 2027 and 7 percent in 2028? Is the reported 10 percent increase in South Korean replacement-parts prices from January 2027 (The Elec via MT Newswires, 12 October 2026) confirmed, and does it extend to other regions and to service contracts? How did ASML's system and service pricing, discounting and gross margin behave in 2009, 2019 and 2023 to 2024 when customers cut capital spending? How are DUV immersion prices behaving in China against domestic tools and yen-priced Japanese tools? Where is the ceiling on High-NA pricing, given TSMC's and Samsung's deferrals on tool cost (about 380 million US dollars a tool) and Intel's adoption? What do volume purchase agreements (up to five years), mandatory down payments and exit costs allow ASML to change in price after an order is placed, and with what lag? How much of rising supplier and input costs has ASML passed through, and how much has it pushed onto suppliers? Focus: How much of the completed value-based price reset will reach gross margin in 2027 and 2028 after capacity-ramp costs, and whether ASML's pricing would hold if capacity tightness eases and customers' cleanroom shortages end in 2028 to 2029.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: ASML's 2026 to 2028 earnings rest on several loops that are reinforcing today but could reverse (cleanroom shortages driving near-100 percent-margin upgrades, customer down payments funding buybacks, and Chinese domestic tools interacting with export controls), and the Analyst must judge which strengthen and which turn balancing within the forecast horizon. Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (installed-base service and upgrade loops, China substitution, prior capacity-cycle reversals); ASM International (content-per-node loop and China mature-node pre-buying); BE Semiconductor (multiple de-rating ahead of an adoption date); Shanghai Aishengna, SMEE and Huawei-linked tool programmes (private; the domestic substitution loop); Carl Zeiss SMT (private; the supplier-capacity loop). Inputs: Upgrade-for-cleanroom loop: how far are customers buying productivity software and field upgrades because new cleanrooms are not ready, how large is that revenue (Installed Base Management 2.8 billion EUR in Q2 2026, 3.3 billion EUR in Q3, about 3.7 billion EUR guided for Q4), and what happened to upgrade demand in past cycles when new capacity arrived? Prepayment-to-capital-return loop: how much of ASML's 2026 cash and buybacks (about 6.1 billion EUR of treasury-share purchases in the first nine months of 2026 against a 12 billion EUR programme for 2026 to 2028) is funded by customer down payments, and how did contract liabilities, inventory and buybacks behave in 2019 and 2023 when orders slowed? Lead-time-to-booking loop: do long lead times (12 to 24 months) produce precautionary or duplicate orders, how effective are ASML's down-payment and exit-cost controls, and what precedent does the 2021 to 2022 shortage and 2023 to 2024 correction give? China loop: does each step in Chinese domestic immersion DUV (mass production from July 2026; planned shipments for 2026 and 2027) raise the probability of the MATCH Act or equivalent bans, and does each ban accelerate domestic qualification; what is the evidence from deposition and etch, where substitution has gone further? Installed-base flywheel: does a growing installed base fund R&D that extends node leadership and grows the installed base further, and at what rate? High-NA ecosystem loop: do the 12-inch photomask initiative (ASML, TSMC, Samsung; announced September 2026), Intel's volume use and Samsung's and SK hynix's 2028 DRAM plans reinforce High-NA adoption, or does Low-NA multi-patterning optimisation keep delaying it? Financing loop: how does stress in AI-infrastructure financing (neocloud credit spreads, hyperscaler free cash flow near zero in 2026 to 2027 per Jefferies, 15 October 2026) transmit to semiconductor capital equipment orders and multiples, and how did similar loops end in the telecom build-out of 1998 to 2002? Focus: Which of these loops are most likely to turn from reinforcing to balancing between 2027 and 2029, in what order, and with what observable early signals, so the Analyst can shape the margin, cash and unit paths accordingly.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Consensus EPS for 2028 is widely dispersed among brokers and management has not quantified several of its main inputs (2028 units, the size of the price reset, the normalised upgrade run-rate, the 2027 margin), so the Analyst needs evidence on each input of the EPS bridge. Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (how revenue mix, margin, tax and buybacks drove EPS through prior up-cycles and downturns); ASM International (comparable Dutch equipment maker with an unhedged-currency and capitalised-development profile); BE Semiconductor (buyback and payout precedent in a cyclical equipment business). Inputs: Low-NA EUV units and ASPs for 2027 and 2028 (company capacity statements: about 85 systems in 2027; a 30 percent increase for 2028 "will not meet demand", 14 October 2026; Morgan Stanley's reading of more than 110 systems, 15 October 2026; Deutsche Bank on module-build dependencies in Wilton and Taiwan); High-NA EXE units, prices and gross margin for 2026 to 2028; DUV immersion units (about 130, 170 and 220 capacity in 2026 to 2028 per company statements) and the China share of system sales (14 percent in Q2 2026, 23 percent in Q3; about 20 percent of 2026 revenue guided); Installed Base Management split into service, hardware upgrades and productivity software, and the expected path of each; the gross-margin bridge for 2027 and 2028 (pricing, EUV mix, upgrade normalisation, High-NA dilution, capacity-ramp costs, NXE:3800F and 4200G timing); R&D and SG&A growth; depreciation and amortisation including capitalised development and new capacity; the effective tax rate under the Dutch innovation box and Pillar Two; net interest income; the size and pace of buybacks after the 12 billion EUR programme and the dividend policy; the treatment of the Mistral AI stake (a 509.7 million EUR revaluation gain in Q3 2026); the behaviour of contract liabilities and working capital. For each, what the company guides, what brokers assume (Bernstein 2028 EPS 84 EUR; Morgan Stanley 68.47 EUR; Redburn about 91 EUR diluted; Jefferies 2027 EPS 60.77 EUR with a flat 2028 gross margin, all October 2026), how far apart they are, and what would move the input materially either way. Focus: Which two or three inputs explain most of the gap between the low and high 2028 EPS estimates, and what evidence, expected at the 27 January 2027 results and the 10 June 2027 Capital Markets Day, would narrow it.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 00:31 to 00:46 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
ASML (ASML-NL): answers to the second set of questions (financial forecasts)

Written 2026-10-25 for the Analyst answering the third set (predictability of the 18-month forecasts) and the fourth set (how far guidance, consensus and the market narrative reflect them).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

Conventions. Money in EUR millions unless stated; per-share figures in EUR. GM is gross margin. IBM is Installed Base Management (service, field upgrades and productivity software). CL is contract liabilities (customer down payments). "Low-NA" is the NXE EUV platform; "High-NA" is the EXE platform. EBITDA and EBIT are on the FactSet operating basis used in the financials block (FY2025 EBITDA 12,411, EBIT 11,301, D&A 1,110); this basis sits within 0.5% of the brokers' adjusted basis in FY2025, so my figures can be read against consensus directly. EPS is reported basic (FY2025 24.73, identical to the brokers' basis).

My base case in one table (the forecast-inputs form carries the full detail).

The base case is 1 to 3% below consensus EPS in FY2027 and FY2028 and 16% below the thin FY2029 consensus. The gap is not a margin disagreement: my EBIT margins (44.8% and 47.2%) match consensus (44.9% and 47.0%). It is a revenue disagreement concentrated in three places: China DUV in 2027 to 2028, the timing of High-NA revenue, and a 2029 slowdown that consensus does not carry.

Where I disagree with the first set, and why (the Consumer should carry these corrections).

  1. The "FY2027 consensus is below the Q4 2026 run-rate" argument overstates the cushion. Q4 is ASML's seasonally largest quarter: it was 32.8% of FY2024 revenue, 29.7% of FY2025 and will be about 33% of FY2026. Annualising Q4 2024 (9,263 x 4 = 37.1 billion) overstated FY2025 revenue (32.7 billion) by 13%. The correct yardstick is H2 2026 annualised (11,662 + 14,800 = 26.5 billion, so 52.9 billion) plus unit and price growth. On that yardstick FY2027 consensus (57.5 billion) is achievable but not undemanding: it needs DUV systems growth of about 25% while China falls back.
  2. Reported quarterly growth will decelerate sharply through 2027 even in my base case. Year-on-year growth runs about +52% (Q4 2026), +44% (Q1 2027), +44% (Q2), +20% (Q3) and +8% (Q4 2027), purely because the 2026 comparison base steps up. The first set placed the turn of the second derivative in EPS growth; in reported quarterly revenue it arrives in Q3 2027, and that is the print the market will read as a peak signal.
  3. The retention award is a P&L cost the first set did not count. The conditional 20,000 EUR award (about 900 million EUR in aggregate, granted 1 January 2027, vesting 1 January 2030) is share-based pay that will be expensed at roughly 300 million EUR a year across 2027 to 2029 if it vests as designed. It roughly cancels the 200 million EUR delayering saving (ING) that the first set treated as the cost lever.
  4. A MATCH Act ban is milder in 2027 to 2028 than the 15%-of-revenue framing implies, and harsher later. While immersion capacity is short of demand (management: +30% "will not meet customer demand"), immersion tools freed from banned Chinese fabs are largely re-allocated to other customers. The non-reallocable loss is Chinese servicing and upgrades (IBM) and Chinese demand above what the rest of the world can absorb. I put the MATCH hit at about 8 to 10% of FY2027 EPS, not the roughly 30% in Jefferies' MATCH case; the loss becomes larger in a 2029 slowdown, when no other customer is waiting for the freed tools.
  5. The share count falls faster than the first set's lower bound only if buybacks are sized to cash. My base has the count falling from about 380.9 million (end-2026) to about 364 million (end-2028), about 2.2% a year, because I size buybacks to free cash flow, which is what ASML has done since 2025 (FY2025 returns about 8.4 billion EUR on FCF of 8.5 billion).

My free-cash-flow definition (used throughout). Operating cash flow less capital spending on fixed and intangible assets, before finance-lease principal. It includes the change in contract liabilities (down payments). This is close to FactSet's definition (which deducts fixed-asset capex only).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

ASML reports two revenue lines, Net System Sales and Installed Base Management (IBM), and gives technology splits (EUV, DUV immersion, DUV dry and KrF, i-line, metrology and inspection) for system sales. It does not report segment margins; the margin comments below are my estimates.

EURm FY2025 actual FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
EUV systems (Low-NA and High-NA) 11,603 16,871 23,186 33,260 35,920 41,260
of which High-NA not disclosed about 1,600 1,850 3,120 4,000 5,740
Non-EUV systems (immersion, dry, KrF, i-line, metrology) 12,871 15,445 18,600 21,400 20,100 20,700
Net system sales 24,474 32,316 41,786 54,660 56,020 61,960
Installed Base Management 8,193 12,239 14,200 15,000 15,600 16,800
Total revenue 32,667 44,555 55,986 69,660 71,620 78,760
Group gross margin (FactSet basis) 51.6% 55.7% 57.6% 58.3% 58.0% 58.8%

Margin by line (estimates): Low-NA EUV systems carry above-group gross margins and rise with value-based pricing and NXE:3800F mix; High-NA runs at about 20 to 25% gross margin in 2026 to 2027 (Morgan Stanley's estimate of about 20%), about 35% in 2028 and about 45% by 2029 to 2030 as volume absorbs fixed costs; DUV immersion is close to the group average, with China and Japanese competition capping price; IBM is the highest-margin line, with productivity software near 100% and service in the 50 to 60% range, so its mix (upgrades about 12% of group revenue in 2026, falling to about 7 to 9% by 2028 to 2029) is the main swing factor in the group gross margin.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Low-NA EUV systems recognised: 65 (2026), 84 (2027; capacity about 85, fixed by long-lead parts and close to fully ordered), 110 (2028; the company's earlier plan; management says +30% is insufficient but has not given a number, and module-build, specialist suppliers and installation staff are the constraints), 112 (2029, a pause), 120 (2030).
  • Low-NA blended ASP: about 235 million EUR (2026), 254 (+8%: about half like-for-like value-based pricing, half NXE:3800E and early 3800F mix), 274 (+8%: full pricing effect and more 3800F), then +4% a year.
  • High-NA: about 5, 5, 8, 10 and 14 systems at 360 to 410 million EUR; Intel and DRAM (Samsung and SK hynix from 2028) carry it; TSMC logic about 2030.
  • DUV immersion: about 130 (2026), 160 (2027), 190 (2028) systems recognised against capacity of about 130, 170 and up to 220; China falls from about 20% of group revenue (2026) to about 15% (2027) and 12 to 13% (2028); China DUV pricing flat.
  • IBM: service grows 12 to 15% a year with the fleet and a 5 to 10% parts and service price rise from 2027; upgrades held near the 2026 run-rate in 2027 (about 6.4 billion EUR) then falling to about 6.0 (2028) and 5.0 billion (2029) as customers' cleanrooms open.
  • Gross margin: pricing adds about 1.3 points (2027) and 2.5 points (2028) against 2026 gross; upgrade normalisation (-1.3 points by 2028), High-NA (-1.0 to -1.2 points, roughly unchanged) and capacity-ramp and 3800F launch costs (-0.5 to -0.8 points in 2027) absorb about half.
  • Operating expenses: 6.24 billion EUR (2026), +14.6% (2027, including about 300 million EUR a year of retention-award share-based pay for 2027 to 2029 and R&D programme growth; the delayering saving of 100 to 200 million EUR from May 2027 is absorbed), +8%, +4.5%, +4%.
  • EBITDA margin follows from the above (EBIT margin 41.7%, 44.8%, 47.2%, 46.7%, 48.1%) plus D&A, which rises with capacity, capitalised development and, from 2029, the Brainport campus.
  • Net financial items: interest income on net cash of about 13 to 27 billion EUR plus the Zeiss SMT equity result; FY2026 includes the 509.7 million EUR Mistral AI revaluation gain (Q3 2026).
  • Tax: about 17% guided for 2026, lowered to 16.6% by the Mistral gain (my inference: exempt under the Dutch participation exemption); 17.5% while the innovation-box ruling runs (2024 to 2028); 18% from 2029 for renewal risk.
  • Buybacks: sized to free cash flow less dividends; the 12 billion EUR 2026 to 2028 programme is about half used and will be exhausted around mid-2027, so I assume a larger programme from the June 2027 Capital Markets Day. Share count other than buybacks: employee-plan deliveries and, in 2030, the vesting of the retention award (about 0.6 million shares).
  • Working capital: on the financials block's definition (receivables plus inventories less payables), which excludes contract liabilities. It ended FY2025 at 35.8% of revenue (seasonally low after Q4 collections and 6.2 billion EUR of factored receivables) and stood at 54.8% at Q3 2026; I assume a Q4 release to 41.5% and gradual improvement from tool cycle-time reduction, with a rise to 43% in the 2029 pause. Important for the model: ASML's customer down payments (contract liabilities) are a separate, large cash source that this definition does not capture. My path for year-end contract liabilities is about 18.9 billion EUR (FY2025), 25.5 (FY2026), 30.0 (FY2027), 32.0 (FY2028), 28.0 (FY2029) and 31.0 (FY2030), so cash inflows of +6.6, +4.5, +2.0, -4.0 and +3.0 billion EUR. If the model has no contract-liability line, its free cash flow will be lower than mine by those amounts in each year (and higher in FY2029).
  • Lease payments: ASML reports under US GAAP, so operating-lease costs sit inside EBITDA; only finance-lease principal is a separate outflow, small and not disclosed in the sources; the figures are my estimates.
  • Capex: above consensus (1,862, 2,209 and 2,400 million EUR for FY2026 to FY2028) because the more-than-30% 2028 capacity build, module-build sites and the Brainport campus (first phase 2029) must be paid for; still under 4.5% of revenue.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance (14 October 2026): revenue 44 to 45 billion EUR, gross margin about 56%; Q4 revenue 14.2 to 15.2 billion EUR, Q4 gross margin about 57%, Q4 IBM about 3.7 billion EUR; China about 20% of revenue; EUV systems growth above 45%, non-EUV about +20%, IBM about +50%. My inputs: revenue 44,555 million EUR (Q4 14.8 billion, slightly above the midpoint, in line with the record of beating the midpoint by 2 to 3%), gross margin 55.7% (FactSet basis; about 56% company basis), Q4 IBM 3.7 billion, EUV +45.4%, non-EUV +20%, IBM +49%. In line.
  • FY2027: no formal guidance until 27 January 2027. Management statements: Low-NA capacity about 85, immersion about 170; pricing benefit partial in 2027; upgrades to continue; capacity-ramp costs, High-NA mix and NXE:3800F reliance offset part of the margin gain; no commitment that 2027 gross margin exceeds the Q4 2026 exit rate (about 57%). My inputs (revenue +25.7%, gross margin 57.6%) are consistent with all of these. I expect the January guide to be a range of roughly 52 to 58 billion EUR with gross margin of 56 to 58%, given ASML's habit of conservative opening guides.
  • Mid-term (2030 framework, November 2024, reiterated): revenue 44 to 60 billion EUR, gross margin 56 to 60%. My FY2030 revenue (78.8 billion EUR) is 31% above the top of the range; my gross margin (58.8%) is inside it. The framework is stale and will be updated at the 10 June 2027 Capital Markets Day.
  • Against consensus (FactSet median, 25 October 2026): revenue 44,464 / 57,507 / 73,683 / 83,599 million EUR against mine 44,555 / 55,986 / 69,660 / 71,620 (+0.2%, -2.6%, -5.5%, -14.3%); EBIT margin 41.8 / 44.9 / 47.0% against mine 41.7 / 44.8 / 47.2%; EPS 42.49 / 57.55 / 76.44 / 91.72 against mine 42.7 / 56.1 / 74.9 / 77.4. My revenue is lower on China DUV, High-NA timing and a 2029 pause; my EPS gap is smaller because I assume larger buybacks than consensus's implied share count (about 376 million in FY2028 against my 368.8 million weighted average).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • ASML reports to the market under US GAAP (statutory accounts under IFRS capitalise and amortise development costs differently; the IFRS H1 2026 gross margin was 50.9% against 53 to 54% under US GAAP). Consensus and these inputs are on the US GAAP basis.
  • EPS: brokers' adjusted EPS equals reported basic EPS in FY2025 (24.73 EUR on both). For FY2026 the only reconciling item is the 509.7 million EUR Mistral AI revaluation gain (Q3 2026, below operating profit, about 1.33 EUR a share): my reported EPS is 42.7 EUR and my underlying EPS is 41.4 EUR. The FactSet median (42.49 EUR) appears to include the gain and the mean (41.19 EUR) appears not to. I do not adjust out the 2027 delayering severance or the retention-award expense; brokers are unlikely to.
  • EBITDA: FactSet operating EBITDA (12,411 million EUR in FY2025) is within 0.4% of the brokers' basis (12,356 million); my EBITDA margins are on the FactSet operating basis and comparable with consensus (44.2%, 47.5%, 48.8% for FY2026 to FY2028).
  • Gross margin: the FactSet annual FY2025 gross margin (51.6%) is below the company's 52.8%; the 2026 quarterly figures align (Q3 57.4% FactSet against 57.5% company). My gross margins are on the FactSet basis and run within about 0.3 points of the company basis for 2026 onward.
  • Free cash flow: FactSet defines FCF as operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex; mine deducts intangible capex too (small) and includes contract-liability changes, as operating cash flow does.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: ASML Holding N.V. (ASML-NL; ASML), the Dutch sole supplier of EUV lithography systems and leading supplier of DUV immersion and dry lithography, metrology and inspection tools; it reports two lines, Net System Sales (EUV Low-NA and High-NA, DUV, metrology and inspection) and Installed Base Management (service, field upgrades and productivity software for the installed fleet).

Request: T-D1

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (the WFE oligopoly; their capacity ramps, installed-base upgrade businesses and guidance records through the 2021 to 2022 shortage and the 2023 to 2024 digestion are the closest dated precedents); ASM International (Dutch front-end peer running a capacity build in Arizona and Korea and a GAA-driven content cycle); BE Semiconductor (capital-light equipment maker whose margins held through an eleven-quarter downturn, and whose valuation fell when an adoption date slipped); Canon and Nikon (lithography competitors; Nikon's precision segment is loss-making and planning a mask-compatible immersion second source); Carl Zeiss SMT (private sole supplier of ASML's optics, whose capacity gates ASML's output); Shanghai Aishengna and SMEE (private, state-backed Chinese immersion DUV entrants). Operating conditions: a supply-constrained up-cycle with customers pre-paying 35 to 40% of order value (2021 to 2022, and 2026 onward); a post-up-cycle digestion with flat or falling tool demand (2019, 2023 to 2024); a memory-led demand peak followed by a memory price break; new-product transitions (NXE:3800E to 3800F to 4200G; High-NA EXE:5000 to 5200); export-control tightening on DUV sales and servicing in China (the proposed MATCH Act); stress in the financing of AI data-centre capital spending. Inputs: Can ASML raise Low-NA EUV output from about 65 systems (2026) to about 85 (2027) and then by more than 30% in 2028, as its statements of 15 July and 14 October 2026 imply, given module-build capacity in Wilton and Taiwan, Zeiss optics, specialist suppliers and installation staff; how many capital-equipment makers have raised unit output by 30% or more a year for two to three consecutive years, how many met their plans, and what distinguished them? Has ASML ever expanded gross margin while ramping capacity this fast, given that it guided Q4 2026 gross margin to about 57% after 57.5% in Q3 on capacity-ramp costs? Does ASML's record support the claim that value-based price increases (negotiations completed October 2026; partial benefit in 2027, full from 2028) will reach gross margin rather than be absorbed by capacity and supplier costs? Can Installed Base Management keep growing after about 50% growth in 2026 (Q3 2026 3.29 billion EUR; Q4 guided about 3.7 billion EUR), and what happened to upgrade revenue at ASML and peers when customers' new cleanrooms came online in past cycles? On what volume path have new lithography platforms (NXE:3300 in 2013 to 2014; EXE now, estimated at about 20% gross margin by Morgan Stanley) reached corporate-average margins? What does ASML's guidance record (the July 2022 growth cut on supply; the October 2024 range and margin cut; three raises in 2026) say about its ability to deliver a supply-constrained ramp? Focus: Whether ASML's demonstrated strengths (pricing, supplier control, execution) are enough to deliver both the 2028 output step and the flow-through of value-based pricing to gross margin at the same time, or whether either depends on supply-chain and installation capacity it has not yet shown it can build.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (dispersion of their earnings outcomes through the 2019, 2021 to 2022 and 2023 to 2024 cycles); ASM International (Dutch peer that declared its 2027 target outdated in 2026); BE Semiconductor (earnings and valuation driven by one adoption date); Nikon and Canon (lithography competitors in mature nodes and packaging); Carl Zeiss SMT (private; the supply-side single point of failure); Shanghai Aishengna, SMEE and Huawei-linked tool programmes (private; the China substitution driver). Operating conditions: a supply-capped ramp with orders reaching 2028 and customer interest in 2029 to 2030; a cleanroom-constrained period in which customers buy productivity upgrades instead of new tools, and the period after those cleanrooms open; a statutory US ban on DUV immersion shipments and servicing to designated Chinese fabs, with and without spare global immersion capacity; a memory price break from 2027 onward; a tightening of AI-infrastructure credit (hyperscaler free cash flow near zero in 2026 to 2027 per Jefferies, 15 October 2026; neocloud bond stress in October 2026). Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers that separate ASML's 2026 to 2029 financials from 2019 to 2025: Low-NA EUV unit output, the size and timing of value-based pricing, the path of productivity upgrades within Installed Base Management, China DUV revenue and servicing, and High-NA adoption timing (Intel in volume; Samsung and SK hynix DRAM from 2028; TSMC about 2030)? How wide is the published range of 2028 forecasts (Morgan Stanley 2028 EPS 68.47 EUR, Bernstein 84 EUR, Redburn about 91 EUR diluted, all October 2026; FactSet median 2028 revenue 73,683 million EUR on 25 October 2026) and which inputs explain most of it? How seasonal are ASML's quarterly revenues (Q4 share of the year; Q1 decline from Q4) and how did that seasonality affect forecasts made by annualising a single quarter? How dispersed were outcomes for semiconductor-equipment companies in the two years after a supply-constrained peak (2001, 2009, 2012 to 2013, 2019, 2023), and how often were forecasts resting on one or two drivers (units and price) met? What evidence exists that productivity-upgrade revenue is cyclical, and how fast it fell at ASML or peers when capacity tightness eased? What share of ASML's China revenue and installed-base servicing comes from entities that the MATCH Act would designate? Focus: Which two or three drivers account for most of the range of plausible ASML outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed that range is (supply caps the upside; volume, upgrade mix and policy combine on the downside), and how wide it becomes for 2028 and 2029.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (latest quarterly results and guidance in October 2026 as read-across on WFE demand, upgrade and service revenue, and China); ASM International (results of 27 October 2026; its 2027 target declared outdated); BE Semiconductor (Q3 2026 backlog and guidance); TSMC, Samsung, SK hynix and Micron (2027 capital-spending guidance as the demand input); Nikon and Canon (lithography order commentary). Operating conditions: a supply-constrained up-cycle in which the company guides output it controls; the run-up to a Capital Markets Day that will reset a stale long-range framework; a US policy calendar with dated decision points (expiry of the Affiliates Rule suspension and of China's rare-earth suspension on 10 November 2026; the MATCH Act in the year-end defence bill); a risk-off market regime for semiconductor equipment in October 2026. Inputs: How has ASML set its opening annual guidance in past January results against the eventual out-turn (for example January 2026: 34 to 39 billion EUR, raised to 44 to 45 billion by October 2026), and against consensus at the time? What is the market likely to expect from the 27 January 2027 results: FY2027 revenue and gross-margin guidance (FactSet median FY2027 revenue 57,507 million EUR and EPS 57.55 EUR on 25 October 2026), the annual backlog (last disclosed 38.8 billion EUR at end-2025; Bernstein inferred about 56 billion EUR on 19 October 2026), Installed Base Management guidance and any 2028 capacity number? What will the 10 June 2027 Capital Markets Day need to show to replace the 2030 framework (44 to 60 billion EUR revenue, 56 to 60% gross margin, November 2024), and what do brokers expect it to contain? How large and broad have consensus revisions been (FactSet FY2027 EPS +7% in one month, +37% in six and +79% in twelve, October 2026), and what has driven past reversals of revision momentum at ASML (2022, 2024)? Where does consensus sit against the company's run-rate after allowing for seasonality, and against the Q4 2026 guide (14.2 to 15.2 billion EUR revenue, about 57% gross margin)? What would cause guidance to be cut (supply shortfalls, customer push-outs, export controls) or raised (upgrades, pricing, more capacity), and what does each require? Focus: Whether the FY2027 guide in January 2027 is likely to be set below, at or above consensus given ASML's record of conservative opening guides, and what the January backlog, Installed Base Management guide and June 2027 Capital Markets Day need to show for consensus FY2027 and FY2028 to be upgraded rather than cut.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Applied Materials, Lam Research, KLA and Tokyo Electron (how the market discusses upgrade cycles, China exposure and cycle peaks for each); ASM International and BE Semiconductor (European equipment names whose valuations fell in 2026 on peak-cycle and adoption-date debates); VAT Group and other ASML suppliers (second-order exposure to ASML's capacity plan); Nikon and Canon (lithography competitors). Operating conditions: a risk-off regime for semiconductor equipment in October 2026 in which the market priced the financing of AI capital spending as the weak link; the sector's pattern of de-rating three to six months before the revenue peak; a crowded long position (76% of covering brokers rate ASML Buy, October 2026); a US export-control debate in the year-end defence bill. Inputs: the cyclicality of productivity-upgrade revenue inside Installed Base Management, and what happens to it when customers' new cleanrooms open; the reliance of ASML's buybacks on customer down payments and what a fall in contract liabilities would signal; whether immersion tools no longer shippable to designated Chinese fabs under the MATCH Act would be re-allocated to other customers while immersion capacity is short of demand, and how much of the China exposure is servicing rather than tools; the sharp deceleration in ASML's reported year-on-year quarterly growth through 2027 caused by the high 2026 comparison base, and the size of the usual Q1 decline from Q4; the size of the completed value-based price increase and how much reaches gross margin after capacity-ramp costs; the expense of the conditional 20,000 EUR employee share award (granted 1 January 2027, vesting 1 January 2030); the 2028 Low-NA output step and the gap between company statements and the 120 to 125 systems some investors hoped for (Morgan Stanley, 15 October 2026); High-NA timing at TSMC, Samsung and SK hynix; the probability and timing of a 2029 digestion year; the AI-financing loop from neocloud credit to equipment orders. Focus: Which of these dynamics are already well known and debated by sell-side analysts and investors, which are discussed by only one or two, and which are discussed by no one, separately for the next 18 months and for 18 months to three years out.
